**Kazakhstan**,[^cite_note-18] officially the **Republic of Kazakhstan**,[^cite_note-19] is a [landlocked country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked_country) situated primarily in [Central Asia](6742.md), with [a portion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Kazakhstan) of its territory extending into [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe).[^cite_note-21] It borders [Russia](25391.md) to the [north and west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Russia_border), [China](5405.md) to the [east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Kazakhstan_border), [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md) to the [southeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Kyrgyzstan_border), [Uzbekistan](31853.md) to the [south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Uzbekistan_border), and [Turkmenistan](198149.md) to the [southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Turkmenistan_border), and it has a coastline along the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md). The capital is [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana), and the country's [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_populous_cities_in_Kazakhstan) and principal cultural and economic center is [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty), which served as the capital until 1997.

Kazakhstan is the world's [ninth-largest country by land area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) and the largest landlocked country. [Hilly plateaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe) and plains account for nearly half its vast territory, with [lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upland_and_lowland) composing another third; its southern and eastern frontiers are composed of mountainous regions. Kazakhstan has a population of 20.6 million and [one of the lowest population densities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population_density) in the world, with fewer than 8 people per square kilometre (21 people/mi<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-22] Ethnic [Kazakhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs) constitute a majority, while [ethnic Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Kazakhstan) form a significant minority. Officially secular, Kazakhstan is a [Muslim-majority country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) with a sizeable [Christian community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Kazakhstan).

Kazakhstan has been inhabited since the [Paleolithic era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic). In antiquity, various nomadic [Iranian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_peoples) such as the [Saka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka), [Massagetae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massagetae), and [Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythians). [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) nomads entered the region from the sixth century. In the 13th century, the area was subjugated by the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) under [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan). Following the disintegration of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) in the 15th century, the [Kazakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate) was established over an area roughly corresponding with modern Kazakhstan. By the 18th century, the Kazakh Khanate had fragmented into three *[jüz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%BCz)* (tribal divisions), which were gradually absorbed and conquered by the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire); by the mid-19th century, all of Kazakhstan was nominally under Russian rule.[^cite_note-23] Following the 1917 [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) and subsequent [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War), it became [an autonomous republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Autonomous_Socialist_Soviet_Republic) of the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_SFSR) within the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). Its status was elevated to that of [a union republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) in 1936. The Soviet government settled Russians and other ethnicities in the republic, which resulted in ethnic Kazakhs being a minority during the Soviet era. Kazakhstan was the last [constituent republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union) of the Soviet Union to [declare independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_State_Independence_of_the_Republic_of_Kazakhstan) in 1991 during [its dissolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union).

Kazakhstan dominates Central Asia both [economically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Kazakhstan) and [politically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Kazakhstan), accounting for 60% of the region's GDP, primarily through its [oil and gas industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_and_gas_industry_in_Kazakhstan); it also has vast mineral resources,[^cite_note-time-24] ranking among the highest producers of iron and silver in the world.[^cite_note-25] Kazakhstan also has the highest [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index) ranking in the region. It is a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) constitutional republic;[^cite_note-26] however, its government is authoritarian.[^cite_note-index2010-27][^cite_note-index2022-28] Nevertheless, there have been incremental efforts at democratization and political reform since the resignation of [Nursultan Nazarbayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursultan_Nazarbayev) in 2019, who had led the country since independence. Kazakhstan is a member state of the [United Nations](31769.md) (UN), [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) (WTO),[^cite_note-29] the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO),[^cite_note-30] [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md) (CIS), [Shanghai Cooperation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Cooperation_Organization) (SCO), [Eurasian Economic Union](27290106.md) (EAEU), [Collective Security Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_Security_Treaty_Organization) (CSTO), [Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe](22796.md) (OSCE), [Organization of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) (OIC), [Organization of Turkic States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Turkic_States) (OTS), [International Organization of Turkic Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Organization_of_Turkic_Culture) and Special Guest status with the [Council of Europe Parliamentary Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe).

## Etymology

According to the most widely accepted theory, the word *qazaq* (Kazakh) derives from a Turkic root meaning "free", "independent", or "wanderer"[^cite_note-Zakon-31]. The name Kazakhstan adds the word *[stan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/-stan)*, meaning settlement.

The Turkic word *qazaq* (قازاق) was reliably recorded in 13th–14th century dictionaries, including the Codex Cumanicus[^cite_note-32] and a Mamluk-Kipchak Arabic dictionary published by Martin Houtsma.[^cite_note-33] In these sources, the word meant "unattached", "homeless", "loner", or "exile", and later acquired the meaning "free man".[^cite_note-34]

In [Turko-Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turko-Persian) sources, the term *Özbek-Qazaq* first appeared during the mid-16th century, in the *Tarikh-i-Rashidi* by [Mirza Muhammad Haidar Dughlat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Muhammad_Haidar_Dughlat), a [Chagatayid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khan) prince of [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir), which locates Kazakh in the eastern part of *[Desht-i Qipchaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desht-i_Qipchaq)*.[^cite_note-35] According to [Vasily Bartold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_Bartold), the Kazakhs likely began using that name during the 15th century.[^cite_note-36]

The English word *Kazakh*, meaning a member of the Kazakh people, derives from [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): казах.[^cite_note-37] The native name is *қазақ, *qazaq**. It might originate from the Turkic word verb *qaz-*, 'to wander', reflecting the Kazakhs' [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_nomads) culture.[^cite_note-etym-38] The term *[Cossack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossacks)* is of the same origin.[^cite_note-etym-38]

Though *Kazakh* traditionally referred only to [ethnic Kazakhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs), including those living in China, Russia, Turkey, Uzbekistan, and other neighbouring countries, the term is increasingly being used to refer to any inhabitant of Kazakhstan, including residents of other ethnicities.[^cite_note-39] In the Kazakh language, the country is called *Qazaqstan* in the [Latin script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_script).[^cite_note-40]

### Antiquity

Kazakhstan has been inhabited since the [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic) era.[^cite_note-41] The [Botai culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botai_culture) (3700–3100 BC) is credited with the first domestication of horses. The Botai population derived most of their ancestry from a deeply European-related population known as [Ancient North Eurasians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_North_Eurasian), while also displaying some [Ancient East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Asians) admixture.[^cite_note-42] [Pastoralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoralism) developed during the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic). The population was [Caucasoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_race) during the [Bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) and [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) period.[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44]

The Kazakh territory was a key constituent of the Eurasian trading [Steppe Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe_Route), the ancestor of the terrestrial [Silk Roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road_UNESCO_World_Heritage_Sites). Archaeologists believe that humans first [domesticated the horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication_of_the_horse) in the region's vast steppes. During recent prehistoric times, Central Asia was inhabited by groups such as the possibly Indo-European [Afanasievo culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afanasievo_culture),[^cite_note-45] later early [Indo-Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Iranians) cultures such as [Andronovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronovo_culture),[^cite_note-Beckwith49-46] and later Indo-Iranians such as the [Saka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saka) and [Massagetae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massagetae).[^cite_note-B_68-47][^cite_note-D_37-48] Other groups included the nomadic [Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythians) and the Persian [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) in the southern territory of the modern country. The Andronovo and [Srubnaya cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srubnaya_culture), precursors to the peoples of the [Scythian cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythian_cultures), were found to harbour mixed ancestry from the [Yamnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamnaya_culture) [Steppe herders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Steppe_Herders) and peoples of the Central European Middle Neolithic.[^cite_note-49]

### Turkic period

Main articles: [Turkic migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_migration), [First Turkic Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Turkic_Khaganate), and [Cumania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumania)



Funerary depiction of long-haired Göktürks in the [Kazakh Steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Steppe). Miho funerary couch, c. 570 CE.

[^cite_note-50]

The main [migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_migration) of the [Turkic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) took place between the 5th and 11th centuries, during which Turkic-speaking groups spread across much of Central Asia. Over time, they gradually assimilated or replaced many of the earlier [Iranian-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_languages) populations, transforming Central Asia from a predominantly [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Iranians) region into one largely Turkic-speaking.[^cite_note-51]

Medieval statehood on the territory of present-day Kazakhstan began to emerge with the establishment of the [Turkic Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Turkic_Khaganate) in the mid-6th century. In 630, the [Eastern Turkic Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Turkic_Khaganate) was defeated by the Chinese and incorporated into the [Tang dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty). In 658, the [Western Turkic Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Turkic_Khaganate) suffered a similar fate after its defeat by Tang forces. Turkic political power was later restored when [Kutlug Ilterish Qaghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilterish_Qaghan) revived the Eastern Turkic Khaganate in 682.[^cite_note-bigenckazist-52] On the territory of the former Western Turkic Khaganate, the [Turgesh Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turgesh_Khaganate) emerged in 699 under the leadership of Üch-elik. The Turgesh actively resisted the Arab expansion into Central Asia during the 720s and 730s, but were ultimately defeated in the mid-8th century. During the same period, the Eastern Turkic Khaganate fragmented as a result of internal conflicts and political disunity.[^cite_note-bigenckazist-52]

From the 8th to the early 13th centuries, the territory of Kazakhstan was successively dominated by the [Karluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karluks), [Oghuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Turks), [Kimeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimek_Khanate), and [Kipchaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumania).[^cite_note-bigenckazist-52] In southern Kazakhstan, the Karluks established a new khaganate in 840, although their rulers retained the title of *[yabghu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yabghu)*. They later came under pressure from the [Samanid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanid_Empire) and eventually became associated with the rise of the [Karakhanid state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara-Khanid_Khanate).[^cite_note-bigenckazist2-53] The Oghuz tribes established themselves in the [Aral Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aral_Sea) region and made [Yangikent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangikent) (Dzhankent) their capital. Despite attempts at political consolidation, internal conflicts weakened the [Oghuz state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Yabgu_State).[^cite_note-bigenckazist2-53] Between the 9th and 11th centuries, much of western and central Kazakhstan came under the control of the [Kimek Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimek_Khanate), whose rulers adopted the title of khagan. The Kimek state was eventually conquered by the [Kipchaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kipchaks).[^cite_note-bigenckazist2-53] The Kipchaks, known in East Slavic chronicles as the Polovtsians, became one of the dominant powers of the Eurasian steppe. Their ruling elites played an influential role in the politics of [Khwarazm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazm), and by the 12th century Kipchak cavalry had become a major military force within the [Khwarazmian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire). They contributed significantly to the empire's expansion, including the conquest of the Karakhanid state and neighbouring Central Asian territories. In 1218, however, Khwarazm came into conflict with [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan), leading to the Mongol conquest of the region and its incorporation into the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire).[^cite_note-bigenckazist2-53]

### Golden Horde

Main articles: [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) and [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde)



[Jochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jochi) Mausoleum, [Ulytau Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulytau_Region), Kazakhstan

The history of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) occupies an important place in modern Kazakh historiography. It was within the framework of this state that the formation of the [Kazakh ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs) took place.[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] In contemporary Kazakh historiography, the Golden Horde is regarded as an important part of the historical continuity of Kazakh statehood.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-Sabitov-58] Some Russian historians have also linked the beginnings of Kazakh state formation to the period of the Golden Horde, emphasizing its significant role in this process.[^cite_note-59] Scholars such as [Marie Favereau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Favereau), [Beatrice Forbes Manz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatrice_Forbes_Manz), and Joo-Yup Lee often regard the Kazakhs as one of the successor peoples of the Golden Horde, alongside other heirs of the Jochid Ulus.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] According to Irina Erofeeva, Kazakh nomads viewed the rise and decline of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) and the subsequent development of their own statehood as part of a recurring historical cycle. Kazakh oral tradition preserved this view through stories about rulers of the Jochid Ulus, including [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan), [Jochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jochi), [Batu Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batu_Khan), [Jani Beg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jani_Beg), [Tokhtamysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokhtamysh), [Urus Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urus_Khan), and [Edigu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edigu). Erofeeva argues that Kazakh nomads regarded the Golden Horde as their own state, whose successive revivals never matched its former power, giving rise to an idealised image of the Jochid Ulus that was romanticised in Kazakh society from the 18th to the 20th centuries.[^cite_note-64]

However, [Soviet historiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_in_the_Soviet_Union) significantly shaped interpretations of [Central Asia](6742.md) and the Golden Horde. Kazakh historian Zhaksylyk Sabitov argues that Soviet ideological censorship influenced the study of the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) and the Golden Horde. He notes that [Shoqan Walikhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoqan_Walikhanov) viewed the Kazakhs as descendants of the Turkic and Mongol tribes of the Jochid Ulus and wrote that they "consider themselves as the Tatars of the Golden Horde".[^cite_note-65] Soviet commentators later interpreted this identification as applying primarily to the [Chinggisid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinggisids) elite, while describing its territory as encompassing much of present-day Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-Sabitov-58] Political repression further constrained historical scholarship, while Soviet historians sought to construct "safe" national histories for the Soviet republics. According to Irina Erofeeva, shifting ideological priorities led many Kazakh historians to alter their views or avoid sensitive subjects, including the history of the Golden Horde. [Sanjar Asfendiyarov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjar_Asfendiyarov), arrested and executed in 1938, was denounced in *[Kazakhstanskaya Pravda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstanskaya_Pravda)* as a "Japanese spy" and accused of promoting [Altynsarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Altynsarin) and [Walikhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoqan_Walikhanov) while "replacing Kazakh history with praise of the khans". Similar censorship occurred in other Soviet republics. Auezov's niece believed that [Mukhtar Auezov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhtar_Auezov) and [Kanysh Satbayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanysh_Satbayev) avoided repression partly because they had Russian wives.[^cite_note-Sabitov-58][^cite_note-Kurzman-66] Modern Kazakh historiography has increasingly moved away from these Soviet-era narratives, although they continue to influence some history textbooks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee201910-67][^cite_note-Sabitov-58]



[Batu Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batu_Khan) on the throne of the Golden Horde

At the beginning of the 13th century, the western and northern lands of present-day Kazakhstan, in the eastern *[Desht-i Kipchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desht-i_Kipchak)*, came under Jochi, while the south was incorporated into the [Chagatai Ulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate).[^cite_note-bigenckazist-52] The [Ulus of Jochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulus_of_Jochi) was divided into two domains under Jochi's sons, [Batu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batu_Khan) and [Orda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orda_Khan).[^cite_note-68]<sup>: 209–210 </sup> Although Batu did not distinguish himself as an outstanding military commander, he proved to be a capable administrator and politician. Without undertaking any major military campaigns between 1242 and 1256, he established suzerainty over the [Russian principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_principalities), [Georgia](48768.md), [Armenia](10918072.md), the [Seljuk Sultanate of Rum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Rum), and several regions of [Iran](14653.md). He is also traditionally credited with founding a number of cities of the Golden Horde.[^cite_note-69]



Miniature from a Timurid genealogy manuscript depicting [Mengu-Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mengu-Timur), c. 1405–1409.

[^cite_note-70]

In 1269, at a kurultai on the [Talas River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talas_\(river\)), [Mengu-Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mengu-Timur), [Baraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baraq_Khan), and [Kaidu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaidu) rejected [Kublai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kublai_Khan)'s authority and allied against him to preserve their independence. Upon ascending the throne in 1266/1267, Möngke Temür adopted the title "The Great Khan", and the Golden Horde came to be officially known as the *Ulug Ulus* (lit. 'Great State' in Turkic).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPochekaev201734–35-71] After the death of [Mengu-Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mengu-Timur) in 1280, his elder brother [Tode Mongke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tode_Mongke) succeeded him peacefully but relied heavily on powerful nobles, particularly [Nogai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nogai_Khan). Nogai expanded his influence over [Bulgaria](3415.md), [Vidin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vidin), and [Serbia](29265.md) and maintained independent relations with the Egyptian sultan. In 1287, he backed a coup that replaced Tode Mongke with Töle Buqa, during whose reign Nogai campaigned against the [Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate) and [Hungary](13275.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPochekaev201745–47-72] Under [Özbeg Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96zbeg_Khan) (r. 1313–1341), [Islam](6037917.md) became the state religion and the Golden Horde underwent administrative reforms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPochekaev201769–70-73] His son [Jani Beg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jani_Beg), who succeeded him in 1341, is often regarded in Kazakh oral tradition as the Golden Horde's "Golden Age". He ended nearly a century of conflict with the Ilkhanate by conquering parts of northern Iran, but died during a campaign in 1357, possibly murdered by his son [Berdi Beg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berdi_Beg). His death marked the end of the Golden Age and the beginning of the political instability known as the [Great Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Troubles).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPochekaev201776,_82–83-74][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEErofeeva2021152–153-75]



Coins of [Urus Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urus_Khan) minted at [Sayram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayram_\(city\)), Kazakhstan.

In the late 1350s and early 1360s, the Golden Horde entered a period of civil strife known as the [Great Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Troubles). The rulers of the Blue Horde asserted their independence, and the descendants of [Tuqa-Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuqa-Timur), Jochi's thirteenth son, rose to power. The most prominent among them was [Urus Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urus_Khan).[^cite_note-bigenckazist2-53] Advancing west from the eastern [Desht-i Kipchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desht-i_Kipchak), Urus seized the throne at [Sarai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarai_\(city\)) before returning to the [Ulytau Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulytau_\(range\)) and the Syr Darya region. His sons Qutluq Buqa and [Toktakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toktakia) twice defeated [Tokhtamysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokhtamysh), who sought refuge with [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUskenbay2016213-76] Urus demanded Tokhtamysh's extradition, but Timur refused, leading to conflict. After several defeats, Timur withdrew to [Samarkand](51491.md) and Kesh in 1376–1377. That same year, Tokhtamysh captured Sarai and reunited the Jochid territories under his rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKadyrbaev2009238-77][^cite_note-78] [Tokhtamysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokhtamysh) pursued an active foreign policy. After Mamai's defeat at the [Battle of Kulikovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kulikovo) in 1380, Tokhtamysh invaded Russia and [burned Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Moscow_\(1382\)) in 1382, restoring tribute to the Golden Horde. In 1383, he restored his authority over [Khwarazm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazm), and his growing imperial ambitions soon brought him into conflict with [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur). Supported by the Golden Horde nobility, Tokhtamysh expanded into the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus), subduing the [Jalayirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalayirid_Sultanate) and bringing [Shirvan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirvan) under Horde control. He then invaded the Chagatai ulus, reached [Samarkand](51491.md), and defeated Timur's son [Umar Shaykh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar_Shaikh_Mirza_I) before withdrawing when Timur returned. In retaliation, Timur devastated Khwarazm the following year and incorporated it into his domains.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPochekaev2017117–119-79]



Battle between [Tokhtamysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokhtamysh) (left) and [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur) (right) on 18 June 1391, from the *Zafarnama* ("Book of Victory").

The armies of [Tokhtamysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokhtamysh) and [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur) followed the Turko-Mongol military tradition, with strict discipline and a decimal organisation of tens, hundreds, thousands, and tens of thousands (tumens). [Edigu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edigu), a [Manghit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manghit) hostile to Tokhtamysh, secretly allied with Timur and helped organise his campaign. In 1391, Timur invaded the *Dasht-i Qipchaq* and defeated Tokhtamysh at the [Battle of the Kondurcha River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Kondurcha_River). Despite heavy losses, Timur withdrew with substantial booty, while Tokhtamysh retreated to the [Volga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga) region. By 1393, Tokhtamysh had rebuilt his forces and resumed collecting tribute from his vassals.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFavereau2021286-81]

In 1395, the two armies clashed again at the [Battle of the Terek River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Terek_River). Tokhtamysh initially gained the initiative, repeatedly attacking Timur's [Chagatai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagatai_Khanate) forces before feigning retreat. However, the defection of the emir Aktau, whom Timur had bribed, weakened Tokhtamysh's left flank and allowed Timur to defeat his army. Tokhtamysh escaped but could not raise another army. Timur then devastated the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde), destroying much of its urban infrastructure, trade, and economy. His forces ravaged the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus), Volga region, and [Crimea](163045.md), ultimately forcing Tokhtamysh from the throne in 1395–1396.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPochekaev2017120–123-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMirgaleev200331–33-83][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFavereau2021287-84]

After Tokhtamysh, no ruler achieved authority over the entire Ulus of Jochi, and the Golden Horde began to disintegrate. Timur installed descendants of [Urus Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urus_Khan) in [Sarai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarai_\(city\)), but they failed to restore its former power.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKadyrbaev2009238-77] In 1397, [Edigu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edigu) allied with [Temür Qutlugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tem%C3%BCr_Qutlugh), grandson of Urus Khan, and placed him on the throne while effectively ruling himself. In 1399, Tokhtamysh and his Lithuanian allies attacked Edigu but suffered a crushing defeat. Tokhtamysh later allied with the [Shibanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shibanids) and sought Timur's support, but never regained the throne. Both Tokhtamysh and Timur died in 1405. Soon, the [Timur's empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Empire) began to disintegrate as well.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFavereau2021288-85][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPochekaev2017131–132-86] In 1423–1424, [Baraq Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baraq_Khan), grandson of Urus Khan and son of Qoyrichaq, seized power and captured Sarai after defeating Khudaydat and [Ulugh Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulugh_Muhammad). In 1428, Ulugh Muhammad defeated Baraq, who abandoned attempts to regain power in the west and turned against the descendants of Timur in Samarqand. Аfter Baraq, power in the eastern [Desht-i Kipchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desht-i_Kipchak) passed from the Tuqa-Timurids to the Shaybanids. Baraq's son Abu Sa'id, also known as [Janibek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janibek_Khan), later became a founder of the [Kazakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKadyrbaev2009239–240-87]

### Kazakh Khanate

Main article: [Kazakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate)



Mausoleums of Rabiga Sultān Begim and Khoja Ahmad Yasawi in the city of [Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkistan_\(city\))

By the mid-15th century, the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) had collapsed, leading to the creation of the [Kazakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate) by [Kerei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerei) and [Janibek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janibek_Khan). According to Russian sources, by the 17th century, the Kazakh Khanate was divided into three Zhuzes (Hordes)—Senior (Great), Middle (Central), and Junior—each with its own nomadic territories.[^cite_note-Russian_Encyclopedia-88][^cite_note-Lee-89] In the early 16th century, the Kazakhs transformed [the Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate) into a [nomadic empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic_empire) stretching across the steppes east of the Caspian Sea and north of the [Aral Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aral_Sea) as far as the upper [Irtysh River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irtysh) and the western approaches to the [Altai Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altai_Mountains). During the reigns of [Burunduk Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burunduk_Khan) (ruled 1488–1509) and [Kasym Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kasym_Khan) (1509–18), the Kazakhs were the masters of virtually the entire steppe region, reportedly able to field 200,000 cavalry and feared by all their neighbours. Many historians consider Kasym Khan's leadership the starting point of a distinct and sovereign Kazakh state. His influence extended Kazakh authority from the southeastern regions of modern Kazakhstan to the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains).[^cite_note-Britannica-90][^cite_note-Lee-89]



[Padishah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padishah) (Emperor) of Dast-i Qipchaq, (1550). Possible portrait of [Kazakh khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%B6re_\(dynasty\)).

[^cite_note-91]

However, during the successive rule (1518–1538) of the three sons of Kasym Khan, the power of the Khanate weakened somewhat, accompanied by a trend that later intensified: the disintegration of the Khanate into three separate "hordes". The youngest son of Kasym Khan, Haq-Nazar Khan, who ruled the Kazakh steppes, managed to overcome these difficulties. By restoring the unity of the three Hordes, he began systematic campaigns into [Transoxiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transoxiana). This policy continued under his immediate successors until the reign of Tawakul Khan, who, for a brief time, [seized Samarkand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_invasion_of_Northern_Bukhara).[^cite_note-Britannica-90] Esim Khan succeeded his brother Tawakul Khan. He fought the Bukharan khans Baki-Muhammad and Imamkuli for control of the [Syr Darya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syr_Darya) and [Tashkent](57533.md), seeking to reunify the [Kazakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate). After Tursun Muhammad Khan seized power in the 1610s, Esim retreated to the [Yarkent Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarkent_Khanate), where he ruled over part of the Kyrgyz and allied with Khan Abd ar-Rahim. In the 1620s, he defeated Tursun, restored his authority over the khanate, and suppressed the [Katagan clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katagans), which had supported Tursun. He also defeated the [Oirats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oirats), temporarily bringing them under his rule, and forced Imamkuli to abandon his claims to the cities along the Syr Darya.[^cite_note-92]

From 1635, the Kazakhs began waging war against the newly established [Dzungar Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzungar_Khanate). After Jangir, [Tauke Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tauke_Khan) ascended the throne and distinguished himself as a military commander in wars against the Dzungars and the [Khanate of Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Bukhara). He also pursued an active foreign policy, intervening in succession disputes in [Moghulistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarkent_Khanate), maintaining diplomatic relations with Russia, and sending five embassies there. [Conflicts with the Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_raids_into_Russia) also occurred, including Tauke Khan's suppression of the [Bulavin Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulavin_Rebellion). After Tauke's death in 1715/1718, the Kazakh Khanate lost its unity, and the three hordes effectively became separate khanates.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKadyrbaev20239-94] [^cite_note-Russian_Encyclopedia-88]

In 1723, after concluding a truce with Qing China, [Tsewang Rabtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsewang_Rabtan_Khan) launched a surprise attack on Kazakh lands, beginning the period known as the "Great Disaster".[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKadyrbaev20239-94] Caught unprepared, the Kazakhs retreated, abandoning their livestock and property, while many clans fled across the Emba and Ural rivers and southward into Mawarannahr. This migration towards the [Ural region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_region) became so extensive that it threatened the survival of the [Kalmyk Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalmyk_Khanate).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKadyrbaev20239–10-96] Initially unable to organise effective resistance, the Kazakhs were led by military elites and batyrs, with organised resistance uniting the militias of all three zhuzes under Abulkhair Khan in 1726. Despite Dzungar numerical and military superiority, Kazakh forces defeated the Oirat armies in 1727–1730, preventing Tsewang Rabtan from subjugating the Kazakh zhuzes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKadyrbaev202312-97][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoiseev1991111-98] Under his successor [Galdan Tseren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galdan_Tseren_Khan), Dzungar power reached its height, defeating China in 1731–1732 and subjugating the Senior zhuz in 1735.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoiseev1991128-129,_139-140-99]

From 1752 to 1755, the Middle zhuz fought its final wars with the Dzungars, while [Ablai Sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ablai_Khan) supported different factions and led Middle Zhuz forces in campaigns into Dzungaria.[^cite_note-100] Kazakh intervention and repeated incursions further weakened Dzungar political authority and military cohesion. As a result, when the [Qing Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) invaded in 1755, it encountered little serious resistance and destroyed the Dzungar Khanate as a political entity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoiseev1991232-101]



Approximate areas occupied by the three Kazakh jüz in the early 20th century&#x20;

| [Junior Juz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junior_Juz) | [Middle Juz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Juz) | [Senior Juz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_Juz) |
| --- | --- | --- |

The collapse of the Dzungar Khanate shifted the balance of power in Inner Asia, with the Kazakh khanates becoming the dominant regional political and military force for a time. Meanwhile, [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and [Qing China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) strengthened their positions and began to shape international relations in northwestern Central Asia.[^cite_note-102] Ablai Khan manoeuvred between Russia and China, allowing the Kazakh Khanate to maintain relative independence for a time. Later, Ablai and the Kazakh lords who supported him embarked on a course of military conquest, conducting plundering raids against the population of the [Altai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altai_Mountains), as well as into [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md) and [Central Asia](6742.md). He also co-operated with the Russian authorities in pursuing the Kalmyks who had fled from the Volga, while brutally repressing ordinary members of Kazakh society who were dissatisfied with his policies. He later became the last khan whose authority was recognised throughout the Kazakh steppe. After his death, his son Wali Khan abandoned this independent policy and recognised Russian suzerainty.[^cite_note-Russian_Encyclopedia-88][^cite_note-103]

In 1822, the Khanate institution among the Kazakh [Hordes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuz) was abolished, sparking the [uprising of Sultan Qasym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenesary's_Rebellion) in 1824–1827. The final attempt to restore the Khanate was led by Sultan [Kenesary Qasymov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenesary_Qasymuly), who proclaimed himself khan in 1837 and conducted an armed struggle against Russian authorities until his death in 1847.[^cite_note-Russian_Encyclopedia-88] Assessments of [Kenesary Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenesary_Khan) have varied with changing political circumstances. In Soviet, Russian, and Kazakh historiography between 1917 and 1953, he was portrayed variously as a hero of the [national liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_independence) and [anti-colonial struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-colonialism) or as a "[reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary)". His activities and legacy remain complex and contested. Kenesary's campaigns also inspired [Jules Verne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Verne)'s novel *[Michael Strogoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Strogoff)* (1876).[^cite_note-104]

### Russian Kazakhstan

Main article: [Kazakhstan in the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_in_the_Russian_Empire)

See also: [Kazakh raids into Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_raids_into_Russia)



Portrait of Abulkhair Khan by John Castle



Abulkhair Khan's letter to Empress Anna Ioannovna, 1731

In the first half of the 18th century the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) constructed the [Irtysh line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irtysh_line?action=edit\&redlink=1), a series of forty-six forts and ninety-six redoubts, including [Omsk](62593.md) (1716), [Semipalatinsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semey) (1718), [Pavlodar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlodar) (1720), [Orenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orenburg) (1743) and [Petropavlovsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petropavl) (1752),[^cite_note-105] to prevent Kazakh and Oirat raids into Russian territory.[^cite_note-106] In the late 18th century the Kazakhs took advantage of [Pugachev's Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pugachev's_Rebellion), which was centred on the Volga area, to raid Russian and [Volga German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Germans) settlements.[^cite_note-107] In the 19th century, the Russian Empire began to expand its influence into Central Asia. The "[Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Game)" period is generally regarded as running from approximately 1813 to the [Anglo-Russian Convention of 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Russian_Convention_of_1907). The [tsars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar) effectively ruled over most of the territory belonging to what became the Republic of Kazakhstan.

In the words of A. I. Maksheev, "during the whole of the eighteenth and the beginning of the nineteenth century, the Kazakhs were Russian subjects in name only. In reality, they preserved their independence. The Russians had to separate themselves from them with fortresses and troops and did not even dare to cross the Line." However, this began to change in the 1820s.[^cite_note-108]

Beginning in the late 19th century, the Kazakh national movement sought to preserve the native language and cultural identity in opposition to the Russian Empire's assimilation policies. At the same time, Russian-style education introduced modern ideas to the steppe, and figures like [Shoqan Walikhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoqan_Walikhanov) and [Abay Kunanbayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abai_Qunanbaiuly) adapted these ideas to the specific needs of Kazakh society, creating a secular culture unparalleled in other parts of Asian Russia.[^cite_note-Britannica-90]

From the 1890s onward, ever-larger numbers of settlers from the Russian Empire began [colonizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonization) the territory of present-day Kazakhstan, in particular, the province of [Semirechye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jetisu). The number of settlers rose still further once the [Trans-Aral Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Aral_Railway) from [Orenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orenburg) to [Tashkent](57533.md) was completed in 1906. A specially created Migration Department (Переселенческое Управление) in [St. Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Petersburg) oversaw and encouraged the migration to expand Russian influence in the area. During the 19th century, about 400,000 Russians immigrated to Kazakhstan, and about one million Slavs, Germans, Jews, and others immigrated to the region during the first third of the 20th century.[^cite_note-109] [Vasile Balabanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasile_Balabanov) was the administrator responsible for the resettlement during much of this time.

The competition for land and water that ensued between the Kazakhs and the newcomers caused great resentment against colonial rule during the final years of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire). The most significant uprising, the [Central Asian revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asian_revolt_of_1916), occurred in 1916. The Kazakhs attacked Russian and [Cossack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossacks) settlers and military garrisons. The revolt resulted in a series of clashes and in brutal massacres committed by both sides.[^cite_note-110] Both sides resisted the communist government until late 1919.

In the wake of the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution), the [Alash Orda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alash_Autonomy) government was formed in 1917 as an attempt to secure Kazakh autonomy. Although it existed only in name, Alash Orda represented the Kazakh push for self-rule. The Bolshevik Red Army eventually defeated White Russian forces in the region by 1920, and Kazakhstan was incorporated into the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-Britannica-90]

### Kazakh SSR

Main article: [Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic)

Following the [collapse of central government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) in [Petrograd](24320051.md) in November 1917, the Kazakhs (then in Russia officially referred to as "Kirghiz") experienced a brief period of autonomy (the [Alash Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alash_Autonomy)) before eventually succumbing to the [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks)' rule. On 26 August 1920 the [Kirghiz Autonomous Socialist Soviet Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirghiz_Autonomous_Socialist_Soviet_Republic_\(1920%E2%80%9325\)) within the [Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) (RSFSR) was established. The Kirghiz ASSR included the territory of present-day Kazakhstan, but its administrative centre was the mainly Russian-populated town of [Orenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orenburg). In June 1925, the Kirghiz ASSR was renamed the [Kazak ASSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) and its administrative centre was transferred to the town of [Kyzylorda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyzylorda), and in April 1927 to [Alma-Ata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty).

Despite their traditionally nomadic lifestyle, the Kazakhs were among the most literate groups in Central Asia, which set them apart in the region. However, Soviet policies, including forced [collectivization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivization) and repression of the traditional elite in the late 1920s and 1930s, led to devastating consequences for the Kazakh population. These policies caused widespread famine and high fatalities, with an estimated 1.5 million deaths between 1926 and 1939, mostly due to starvation and related diseases, as well as violence (see also: [Famine in Kazakhstan of 1932–33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine_in_Kazakhstan_of_1932%E2%80%9333)). Thousands of Kazakhs fled to China, but many did not survive the journey.[^cite_note-Britannica-90][^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] During the 1930s, some members of the Kazakh intelligentsia were executed – as part of the [policies of political reprisals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_repression_in_the_Soviet_Union) pursued by the Soviet government in Moscow.

On 5 December 1936 the [Kazakh Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) (whose territory by then corresponded to that of modern Kazakhstan) was detached from the [Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) (RSFSR) and made the [Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic), a full [union republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union) of the USSR, one of eleven such republics at the time, along with the [Kirghiz Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirghiz_Soviet_Socialist_Republic).

The republic was one of the destinations for exiled and convicted persons, as well as for mass resettlements, or deportations affected by the central USSR authorities during the 1930s and 1940s, such as approximately 400,000 [Volga Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Germans) deported from the [Volga German Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_German_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) in September–October 1941, and then later the [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Kazakhstan) and [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_the_Crimean_Tatars). Deportees and prisoners were interned in some of the biggest [Soviet labour camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag) (the Gulag), including [ALZhIR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akmol) camp outside Astana, which was reserved for the wives of men considered "enemies of the people".[^cite_note-113] Many moved due to the policy of [population transfer in the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union) and others were forced into [involuntary settlements in the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Involuntary_settlements_in_the_Soviet_Union).

The [Soviet-German War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)) (1941–1945) led to an increase in industrialization and [mineral extraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_extraction) in support of the war effort. At the time of [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin)'s death in 1953, however, Kazakhstan still had an overwhelmingly agricultural economy. In 1953, Soviet leader [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) initiated the [Virgin Lands Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Lands_Campaign) designed to turn the traditional pasturelands of Kazakhstan into a major grain-producing region for the Soviet Union. The Virgin Lands policy brought mixed results. However, along with later modernizations under Soviet leader [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev) (in power 1964–1982), it accelerated the development of the agricultural sector, which remains the source of livelihood for a large percentage of Kazakhstan's population. Because of the decades of privation, war and resettlement, by 1959 the [Kazakhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs) had become a minority, making up 30% of the population. Ethnic [Russians](59510.md) accounted for 43%.[^cite_note-114]

In 1947, the USSR, as part of its [atomic bomb project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_atomic_bomb_project), founded an [atomic bomb test site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semipalatinsk_Test_Site) near the north-eastern town of [Semipalatinsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semey), where the [first Soviet nuclear bomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RDS-1) test was conducted in 1949. Hundreds of nuclear tests were conducted until 1989 with adverse consequences for the nation's environment and population.[^cite_note-115] The [Anti-nuclear movement in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-nuclear_movement_in_Kazakhstan) became a major political force in the late 1980s.

In April 1961, [Baikonur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baikonur) became the springboard of [Vostok 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vostok_1), a spacecraft with Soviet cosmonaut [Yuri Gagarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Gagarin) being the first human to enter space.

In December 1986, mass demonstrations by young ethnic Kazakhs, later called the [Jeltoqsan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeltoqsan) riot, took place in Almaty to protest the replacement of the [First Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary) of the [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Kazakhstan) of the Kazakh SSR [Dinmukhamed Konayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinmukhamed_Konayev) with [Gennady Kolbin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gennady_Kolbin) from the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_SFSR). Governmental troops suppressed the unrest, several people were killed, and many demonstrators were jailed.[^cite_note-116] In the waning days of Soviet rule, discontent continued to grow and found expression under Soviet leader [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev)'s policy of *[glasnost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost)* ("openness").

### Independence



[Donald Trump](4848272.md) meets with Kassym-Jomart Tokayev in the Oval Office in 2025.

Kazakhstan declared its sovereignty within the Soviet Union on 25 October 1990. Following the failed August 1991 [coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt) in Moscow, the country proclaimed full independence on 16 December 1991, becoming the last Soviet republic to do so. Ten days after Kazakhstan's declaration, the Soviet Union itself dissolved. This period marked a significant turning point in Kazakhstan's history, as it embarked on a new political and economic path separate from Moscow's control.

Nursultan Nazarbayev, the communist-era leader of Kazakhstan, became the country's first president. Under his leadership, Kazakhstan transitioned from a Soviet-era planned economy to a market economy, focusing on privatization and foreign investments. The emphasis on economic reform, particularly in the oil sector, helped Kazakhstan become one of Central Asia's most economically powerful nations. By 2006, Kazakhstan contributed around 60% of the region's GDP, primarily through its oil exports. However, political reforms lagged behind these economic strides, and Nazarbayev maintained an authoritarian rule throughout his presidency.[^cite_note-time-24]

In 1997, Nazarbayev [moved the capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_relocation_in_Kazakhstan) from Almaty, the country's largest city, to Astana (later renamed Nur-Sultan in 2019),[^cite_note-117] a decision that symbolized the government's desire to modernize and assert control over the country's vast territories. The capital city change was part of broader efforts to establish a new national identity and shift the political center from former Soviet-era heartlands.[^cite_note-118]

Kazakhstan's political landscape during Nazarbayev's rule was characterized by limited political pluralism. In the 2004 parliamentary elections, the pro-government Otan Party, led by Nazarbayev, dominated the Majilis (the lower house of parliament). Other parties sympathetic to the president, such as the agrarian-industrial bloc AIST and the Asar party (founded by Nazarbayev's daughter), secured most of the remaining seats, while opposition parties struggled to gain representation. International observers, including the Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe, criticized the elections for not meeting democratic standards.[^cite_note-119]

Despite claims of progress toward democracy, Kazakhstan's political system remained authoritarian well into the 21st century.[^cite_note-120] In 2010, the country was still ranked as an [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) on *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*'s [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist_Democracy_Index).[^cite_note-index2010-27][^cite_note-index2022-28] Nazarbayev, who had ruled since independence, announced his resignation on 19 March 2019, after nearly three decades in power.[^cite_note-121] His successor, [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev), won the 2019 presidential election and took office on 12 June 2019.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123] Tokayev's first official act was to rename the capital city to Nur-Sultan, in honour of Nazarbayev's legacy.[^cite_note-124]

But Tokayev's presidency faced significant challenges. In January 2022, Kazakhstan was gripped by [large-scale protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kazakh_unrest) following a sharp rise in fuel prices.[^cite_note-125] The unrest quickly escalated, and Tokayev responded decisively by assuming control of the country's Security Council, removing Nazarbayev from the post and consolidating his own power. This marked a dramatic shift in Kazakhstan's political dynamics, with Tokayev signalling a departure from the old Nazarbayev-era system.[^cite_note-126] In September 2022, the capital's name was reverted to Astana, a move seen as part of the broader efforts to distance the country from the former president's influence.[^cite_note-127]

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Kazakhstan) and [List of cities in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Kazakhstan)

Since it extends across both sides of the [Ural River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_River), considered the dividing line separating Europe and Asia, Kazakhstan is one of only two [landlocked countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked_country) in the world that [has territory in two continents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcontinental_countries) (the other is [Azerbaijan](746.md)).

With an area of 2,700,000 square kilometres (1,000,000 sq mi)—equivalent in size to Western Europe—Kazakhstan is the ninth-largest country and largest landlocked country in the world. While it was part of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), Kazakhstan lost some of its territory to China's [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) province,[^cite_note-128] and some to Uzbekistan's [Karakalpakstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakalpakstan) autonomous republic during Soviet years.

It shares borders of 6,846 kilometres (4,254 mi) with Russia (the world's longest land border), 2,203 kilometres (1,369 mi) with [Uzbekistan](31853.md), 1,533 kilometres (953 mi) with China, 1,051 kilometres (653 mi) with [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md), and 379 kilometres (235 mi) with [Turkmenistan](198149.md). Major cities include [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty), [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana), [Shymkent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shymkent), [Aktöbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aktobe) and [Karagandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaganda). It lies between latitudes [40°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_parallel_north) and [56° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/56th_parallel_north), and longitudes [46°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/46th_meridian_east) and [88° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/88th_meridian_east). While located primarily in Asia, a [small portion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Kazakhstan) of Kazakhstan is also located west of the [Urals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urals) in Eastern Europe.[^cite_note-129]

Kazakhstan's terrain extends west to east from the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md) to the [Altai Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altai_Mountains) and the [Tian Shan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tian_Shan), and north to south from the plains of [Western Siberia](29639.md) to the oases and deserts of Central Asia. The [Kazakh Steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Steppe) (plain), with an area of around 804,500 square kilometres (310,600 sq mi), occupies one-third of the country and is the world's largest dry [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe) region. The steppe is characterized by large areas of [grasslands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassland) and sandy regions. Major seas, lakes and rivers include [Lake Balkhash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Balkhash), [Lake Zaysan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Zaysan), the [Charyn River and gorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charyn_Canyon), the [Ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ili_River), [Irtysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irtysh_River), [Ishim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishim_River), [Ural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_River) and [Syr Darya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syr_Darya) rivers, and the [Aral Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aral_Sea) until it largely dried up in one of the world's worst environmental disasters.[^cite_note-130]

The [Charyn Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charyn_Canyon) is 80 kilometres (50 mi) long, cutting through a red [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone) plateau and stretching along the Charyn River gorge in northern [Tian Shan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tian_Shan) ("Heavenly Mountains", 200 km (124 mi) east of Almaty) at [43°21′1.16″N 79°4′49.28″E  /  43.3503222°N 79.0803556°E  / 43.3503222; 79.0803556](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Kazakhstan\&params=43_21_1.16_N_79_4_49.28_E_). The steep canyon slopes, columns and arches rise to heights of between 150 and 300 metres (490 and 980 feet). The inaccessibility of the canyon provided a safe haven for a rare [ash tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash_tree), *[Fraxinus sogdiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraxinus_sogdiana)*, which survived the [Ice Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period) there and has now also grown in some other areas.[^cite_note-131] [Bigach crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bigach_crater), at [48°30′N 82°00′E  /  48.500°N 82.000°E  / 48.500; 82.000](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Kazakhstan\&params=48_30_N_82_00_E_), is a [Pliocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliocene) or [Miocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miocene) [asteroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid) [impact crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_crater), 8 km (5 mi) in diameter and estimated to be 5±3 million years old.

Kazakhstan's Almaty region is also home to the [Mynzhylky mountain plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mynzhylky_mountain_plateau).

### Natural resources

See also: [Energy in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Kazakhstan)



[Qarağandy Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara%C4%9Fandy_Region)

Kazakhstan has an abundant supply of accessible mineral and fossil fuel resources. Development of petroleum, natural gas, and mineral extractions has attracted most of the over \$40 billion in foreign investment in Kazakhstan since 1993 and accounts for some 57% of the nation's industrial output (or approximately 13% of gross domestic product). According to some estimates,[^cite_note-Homestead-132] Kazakhstan has the second largest [uranium](31743.md), [chromium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromium), lead, and [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc) reserves; the third largest [manganese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manganese) reserves; the fifth largest copper reserves; and ranks in the top ten for coal, iron, and gold. In 2015, Kazakhstan's gold production is 64 metric tonnes.[^cite_note-133] It is also an exporter of diamonds. Perhaps most significant for economic development, Kazakhstan also has the 11th largest proven reserves of both petroleum and natural gas.[^cite_note-134] One such location is the [Tokarevskoye gas condensate field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokarevskoye_gas_condensate_field).

In total, there are 160 deposits with over 2.7 billion tonnes (2.7 billion long tons) of petroleum. Oil explorations have shown that the deposits on the [Caspian shore](19653787.md) are only a small part of a much larger deposit. It is said that 3.5 billion tonnes (3.4 billion long tons) of oil and 2.5 billion cubic metres (88 billion cubic feet) of gas could be found in that area. Overall the estimate of Kazakhstan's oil deposits is 6.1 billion tonnes (6.0 billion long tons). However, there are only three [refineries](195137.md) within the country, situated in [Atyrau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atirau),[^cite_note-135] [Pavlodar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlodar), and [Şymkent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eymkent). These are not capable of processing the total crude output, so much of it is exported to Russia. According to the US [Energy Information Administration](1704673.md), Kazakhstan was producing approximately 1,540,000 barrels (245,000 m<sup>3</sup>) of oil per day in 2009.[^cite_note-136]



[Lake Kaindy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Kaindy) in south-eastern Kazakhstan



[Aral Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aral_Sea) in 1989 and 2008

There are several large navigable rivers in Kazakhstan. All of them flow along the edges of the country, mainly in the north, while the southern and central parts of the river are mostly steppe, often drying up completely in the height of summer. Kazakhstan also possesses large deposits of [phosphorite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphorite). Two of the largest deposits include the Karatau basin with 650 million tonnes of P<sub>2</sub>O<sub>5</sub> and the Chilisai deposit of the Aktobe phosphorite basin located in northwestern Kazakhstan, with resources of 500–800 million tonnes of 9% ore.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138]

On 17 October 2013, the [Extractive Industries Transparency Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extractive_Industries_Transparency_Initiative) (EITI) accepted Kazakhstan as "EITI Compliant", meaning that the country has a basic and functional process to ensure the regular disclosure of natural resource revenues.[^cite_note-139]

### Climate



Köppen–Geiger climate classification map at 1-km resolution for Kazakhstan 1991–2020

Kazakhstan has an "extreme" [continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate) and [cold steppe climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_steppe), and sits solidly inside the [Eurasian Steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Steppe), featuring the [Kazakh Steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Steppe), with hot summers and cold winters. Indeed, Astana is the second coldest capital city in the world, after [Ulaanbaatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulaanbaatar). [Precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation_\(meteorology\)) varies between arid and semi-arid conditions, the winter being particularly dry.[^cite_note-140]
