| [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) | 30/18 | 86/64 | 0/−8 | 33/17 |
| [Şymkent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eymkent) | 32/17 | 91/66 | 4/−4 | 39/23 |
| [Qarağandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara%C4%9Fandy) | 27/14 | 80/57 | −8/−17 | 16/1 |
| [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana) | 27/15 | 80/59 | −10/−18 | 14/−1 |
| [Pavlodar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlodar) | 28/15 | 82/59 | −11/−20 | 12/−5 |
| [Aqtobe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqtobe) | 30/15 | 86/61 | −8/−16 | 17/2 |

### Wildlife

Main article: [Wildlife of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Kazakhstan)



[Corsac fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsac_fox)

There are ten [nature reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Kazakhstan) and ten [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Kazakhstan) in Kazakhstan that provide safe haven for many rare and endangered plants and animals. In total there are twenty five [areas of conservancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservancy_areas_of_Kazakhstan). Common plants are *[Astragalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astragalus_\(plant\))*, *[Gagea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gagea)*, *[Allium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium)*, *[Carex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carex)* and *[Oxytropis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytropis)*; endangered plant species include native wild apple (*[Malus sieversii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malus_sieversii)*), wild grape (*[Vitis vinifera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitis_vinifera)*) and several wild [tulip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulip) species (e.g., *[Tulipa greigii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_greigii)*) and rare onion species *[Allium karataviense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_karataviense)*, also *[Iris willmottiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_willmottiana)* and *[Tulipa kaufmanniana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_kaufmanniana)*.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] Kazakhstan had a 2019 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 8.23/10, ranking it 26th globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-144]

Common mammals include the [wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf), [red fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox), [corsac fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsac_fox), [moose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose), [argali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argali) (the largest species of sheep), [Eurasian lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_lynx), [Pallas's cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallas's_cat), and [snow leopards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_leopards), several of which are protected. Kazakhstan's Red Book of Protected Species lists 125 vertebrates including many birds and mammals, and 404 plants including fungi, algae and lichens.[^cite_note-145]

On 31 July 2026, a female [Amur tiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_tiger) was released into [Ile-Balkhash State Nature Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ile-Balkhash_State_Nature_Reserve), and three other individuals are still under observational review for later release. This marked the return of the species to the country for the first time since 1948.[^cite_note-146]

[Przewalski's horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Przewalski's_horse) has been reintroduced to the steppes after nearly 200 years.[^cite_note-147]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Kazakhstan), [Politics of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Kazakhstan), and [Elections in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Kazakhstan)



[Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev)\
[President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Kazakhstan)&#x20;



[Oljas Bektenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oljas_Bektenov)\
[Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Kazakhstan)&#x20;

### Political system

Officially, Kazakhstan is a democratic, secular, constitutional [unitary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_republic); Nursultan Nazarbayev led the country from 1991 to 2019.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149] He was succeeded by [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev).[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-tokayev-wins-151] The president may veto legislation that has been passed by the [parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Kazakhstan) and is also the [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [armed forces](16650.md). The prime minister chairs the cabinet of ministers and serves as Kazakhstan's head of government. There are three deputy prime ministers and sixteen ministers in the cabinet.[^cite_note-152]

Kazakhstan has a [unicameral](204304.md) parliament known as the [Kurultai](83119061.md). The Kurultai has 145 members.[^cite_note-153] In 2020, [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) rated Kazakhstan as a "consolidated authoritarian regime", stating that [freedom of speech in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_speech_in_Kazakhstan) is not respected and "Kazakhstan's electoral laws do not provide for [free and fair elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_and_fair_election)."[^cite_note-kaz2-154] The [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) has consistently ranked Kazakhstan as an "authoritarian regime" in its [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index), ranking it 128th out of 167 countries for 2020.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] The [V-Dem Democracy Indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices) described Kazakhstan an [electoral autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_autocracy) in 2024.[^cite_note-j496-157]

### Political reforms

Reforms have begun to be implemented after the election of Kassym-Jomart Tokayev in June 2019. Tokayev supports a culture of opposition, public assembly, and loosening rules on forming political parties.[^cite_note-poleform-158] In June 2019, Tokayev established the National Council of Public Trust as a public platform for national conversation regarding government policies and reforms.[^cite_note-Iran_Daily-159] In July 2019, the president of Kazakhstan announced a concept of a 'listening state' that quickly and efficiently responds to all constructive requests of the country's citizens.[^cite_note-160] A law will be passed to allow representatives from other parties to hold chair positions on some Parliamentary committees, to foster alternative views and opinions. The minimum membership threshold needed to register a political party will be reduced from 40,000 to 20,000 members.[^cite_note-Iran_Daily-159] Special places for peaceful rallies in central areas will be allocated and a new draft law outlining the rights and obligations of organizers, participants and observers will be passed.[^cite_note-Iran_Daily-159] In an effort to increase public safety, President Tokayev has strengthened the penalties for those who commit crimes against individuals.[^cite_note-Iran_Daily-159]

On 17 September 2022, Tokayev signed a decree that limits presidential tenure to one term of seven years.[^cite_note-161] He furthermore announced the preparation of a new reform package to "decentralise" and "distribute" power between government institutions. The reform package also seeks to modify the electoral system and increase the decision-making authorities of Kazakhstan's regions.[^cite_note-\:0-162] The powers of the parliament were expanded at the expense of those of the president, relatives of whom are now also barred from holding government positions, while the Constitutional Court was restored and the death penalty abolished.[^cite_note-\:0-162][^cite_note-163]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Regions of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Kazakhstan) and [Districts of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Kazakhstan)

Kazakhstan is divided into seventeen [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Kazakhstan) ([Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language): облыстар, *oblystar*; Russian: области, *oblasti*), as well as four cities which are administered separately from their surrounding geographic regions.[^cite_note-Republic_of_Kazakhstan-164] The regions are subdivided into 177 [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Kazakhstan) ([Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language): аудандар, *audandar*; Russian: районы, *rayony*).[^cite_note-OECD-165] The districts are further subdivided into rural districts at the lowest level of administration, which include all rural settlements and villages without an associated municipal government.[^cite_note-Adilet-166]

&#x20;The cities of [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty), [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana) and [Shymkent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shymkent) have official status as cities of [republican significance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_republican_significance_\(Kazakhstan\)) and do not belong to their surrounding regions. The city of [Baikonur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baikonur) also has special status because it is being leased to Russia until 2050 for the [Baikonur cosmodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baikonur_cosmodrome).[^cite_note-CIA-167] Shymkent gained its status as a "city of republican significance" in June 2018.[^cite_note-168]

Each region is headed by an *[äkim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akim)* (regional governor) appointed by the president. District *äkimi* are appointed by regional *äkim*s. On 10 December 1997, Kazakhstan's government relocated its capital from Almaty, established under the Soviet Union, to Astana.[^cite_note-169]

### Municipal divisions

Municipalities exist at each level of administrative division in Kazakhstan. Cities of republican, regional, and district significance are designated as urban inhabited localities; all others are designated rural.[^cite_note-Adilet-166] At the highest level are the cities of Almaty and Astana, which are classified as *cities of republican significance* on the administrative level equal to that of a region.[^cite_note-OECD-165] At the intermediate level are *cities of regional significance* on the administrative level equal to that of a district. Cities of these two levels may be divided into city districts.[^cite_note-OECD-165] At the lowest level are *cities of district significance*, and over two-thousand *villages and rural settlements* (*aul*) on the administrative level equal to that of rural districts.[^cite_note-OECD-165]

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Kazakhstan)



President [Nazarbayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursultan_Nazarbayev) with US President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) and Russian President [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev) in 2012

Kazakhstan is a member of the Commonwealth of Independent States, the [Economic Cooperation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Cooperation_Organization) and the [Shanghai Cooperation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Cooperation_Organization). The nations of Kazakhstan, Russia, [Belarus](3457.md), Kyrgyzstan and [Tajikistan](30108.md) established the [Eurasian Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Economic_Community) in 2000, to revive earlier efforts to harmonize trade tariffs and to create a free trade zone under a customs union. On 1 December 2007, it was announced that Kazakhstan had been chosen to chair the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) for the year 2010. Kazakhstan was elected a member of the [UN Human Rights Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Council) for the first time on 12 November 2012.[^cite_note-170]

Kazakhstan is also a member of the United Nations, [Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_for_Security_and_Cooperation_in_Europe), [Euro-Atlantic Partnership Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro-Atlantic_Partnership_Council), [Turkic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_Council), and [Organization of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) (OIC). It is an active participant in the [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization) [Partnership for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partnership_for_Peace) program.[^cite_note-pfpsigs-171]

In 1999 Kazakhstan had applied for observer status at the [Council of Europe Parliamentary Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe_Parliamentary_Assembly). The official response of the Assembly was that because Kazakhstan is partially located in Europe,[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] it could apply for full membership, but that it would not be granted any status whatsoever at the council until its democracy and human rights records improved.

Since independence in 1991, Kazakhstan has pursued what is known as the "multi-vector foreign policy" ([Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language): көпвекторлы сыртқы саясат, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Kazakh): *köpvektorly syrtqy saiasat*), seeking equally good relations with its two large neighbours, Russia and China, as well as with the United States and the rest of the Western world.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175] Russia leases approximately 6,000 square kilometres (2,317 sq mi) of territory enclosing the [Baikonur Cosmodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baikonur_Cosmodrome) space launch site in south central Kazakhstan, where the first man was launched into space as well as Soviet space shuttle [Buran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buran_\(spacecraft\)) and the well-known space station [Mir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir).

On 11 April 2010 presidents Nazarbayev and [Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) met at the Nuclear Security Summit in Washington, D.C., and discussed strengthening the strategic partnership between the United States and Kazakhstan. They pledged to intensify bilateral co-operation to promote nuclear safety and non-proliferation, regional stability in Central Asia, economic prosperity, and universal values.[^cite_note-176]

Since 2014 the Kazakhstani government has been bidding for a non-permanent member seat on the UN Security Council for 2017–2018.[^cite_note-AT5-177] On 28 June 2016 Kazakhstan was elected as a non-permanent member to serve on the UN Security Council for a two-year term.[^cite_note-UN-178]



Kassym-Jomart Tokayev, [Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan), [Xi Jinping](2017814.md), and other leaders at the [Shanghai Cooperation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Cooperation_Organization) summit in Samarkand, 16 September 2022

Kazakhstan has supported UN peacekeeping missions in Haiti, Western Sahara, and Côte d'Ivoire.[^cite_note-TN1-179] In March 2014, the Ministry of Defense chose 20 Kazakhstani military men as observers for the UN peacekeeping missions. The military personnel, ranking from captain to colonel, had to go through specialized UN training; they had to be fluent in English and skilled in using specialized military vehicles.[^cite_note-TN1-179]

In 2014 Kazakhstan gave Ukraine humanitarian aid during the conflict with Russian-backed rebels. In October 2014, Kazakhstan donated \$30,000 to the International Committee of the Red Cross's humanitarian effort in Ukraine. In January 2015, to help the humanitarian crisis, Kazakhstan sent \$400,000 of aid to [Ukraine's southeastern regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novorossiya_\(confederation\)).[^cite_note-gpostukraine1-180] President Nazarbayev said of the war in Ukraine, "The fratricidal war has brought true devastation to eastern Ukraine, and it is a common task to stop the war there, strengthen Ukraine's independence and secure territorial integrity of Ukraine."[^cite_note-ukrainenaz-181] Experts believe that no matter how the Ukraine crisis develops, Kazakhstan's relations with the European Union will remain normal.[^cite_note-CarnegieUkraniepiece-182] It is believed that Nazarbayev's mediation is positively received by both Russia and Ukraine.[^cite_note-CarnegieUkraniepiece-182]

Kazakhstan's Ministry of Foreign Affairs released a statement on 26 January 2015: "We are firmly convinced that there is no alternative to peace negotiations as a way to resolve the crisis in south-eastern Ukraine."[^cite_note-ukrainejan26statement-183] In 2018, Kazakhstan signed the UN [treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons).[^cite_note-184]



President [Kassym-Jomart Tokayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev) with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md), 28 November 2022

On 6 March 2020 the Concept of the Foreign Policy of Kazakhstan for 2020–2030 was announced. The document outlines the following main points:

- An open, predictable and consistent foreign policy of the country, which is progressive in nature and maintains its endurance by continuing the course of the First President – the country at a new stage of development;

- Protection of human rights, development of humanitarian diplomacy and environmental protection;

- Promotion of the country's economic interests in the international arena, including the implementation of state policy to attract investment;

- Maintaining international peace and security;

- Development of regional and multilateral diplomacy, which primarily involves strengthening mutually beneficial ties with key partners – Russia, China, the United States, Central Asian states and the EU countries, as well as through multilateral structures – the United Nations, the Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe, the Shanghai Cooperation Organization, the Commonwealth of Independent States, and others.

  [^cite_note-185]



Member states of the [Collective Security Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_Security_Treaty_Organization) (CSTO)

Kazakhstan's memberships of international organizations include:

- [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md) (CIS)

- [Collective Security Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_Security_Treaty_Organization) (CSTO)

- [Shanghai Cooperation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Cooperation_Organization)

- [Euro-Atlantic Partnership Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro-Atlantic_Partnership_Council)

- [Individual Partnership Action Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_Partnership_Action_Plan), with [NATO](21133.md), Ukraine, Georgia, Azerbaijan, Armenia, [Moldova](19260.md), [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md) and [Montenegro](20760.md)

- [Turkic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_Council) and the [TÜRKSOY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%9CRKSOY) community. (The national language, [Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language), is related to the other [Turkic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages), with which it shares cultural and [historical ties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples))

- United Nations

- [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) (OSCE)

- [UNESCO](21786641.md), where Kazakhstan is a member of its [World Heritage Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Committee)

  [^cite_note-UNESCO-186]

- [Nuclear Suppliers Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Suppliers_Group) as a participating government

- [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization)

  [^cite_note-wtokz-187]

- [Organization of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) (OIC)

  [^cite_note-oicmems-188]

- The [Abraham Accords](64883396.md) (2025)

  [^cite_note-189]

Based on these principles, following Russia's invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, Kazakhstan has increasingly pursued an independent foreign policy, defined by its own foreign policy objectives and ambitions[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] through which the country attempts to balance its relations with "all the major powers and an equally principled aversion towards excessive dependence in any field upon any one of them, while also opening the country up economically to all who are willing to invest there."[^cite_note-192]

Kazakhstan is the 59th most peaceful country in the world, according to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index).[^cite_note-193]

In November 2025, after establishing diplomatic relations with Israel, the Kazakh Foreign Ministry issued a statement indicating that the nation had become a participant in the Abraham Accords to safeguard its interests, consistent with the balanced, constructive, and peaceful foreign policy of the Republic of Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

### Military

Main article: [Armed Forces of the Republic of Kazakhstan](16650.md)



[Kazakhstan Republican Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Guard_\(Kazakhstan\))



A Kazakhstani [Sukhoi Su-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-27)

Most of Kazakhstan's military was inherited from the [Soviet Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Armed_Forces)' [Turkestan Military District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkestan_Military_District). These units became the core of Kazakhstan's new military. It acquired all the units of the [40th Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_Army_\(Soviet_Union\)) (the former 32nd Army) and part of the 17th Army Corps, including six land-force divisions, storage bases, the 14th and 35th air-landing brigades, two rocket brigades, two artillery regiments, and a large amount of equipment that had been withdrawn from over the Urals after the signing of the [Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Conventional_Armed_Forces_in_Europe). Since the late 20th century, the Kazakhstan Army has focused on expanding the number of its armoured units. Since 1990, armoured units have expanded from 500 to 1,613 in 2005.

The Kazakh air force is composed mostly of Soviet-era planes, including 41 [MiG-29s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-29), 44 [MiG-31s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-31), 37 [Su-24s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-24) and 60 [Su-27s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-27). A small naval force is maintained on the Caspian Sea.[^cite_note-196]

Kazakhstan sent 29 military engineers to [Iraq](7515928.md) to assist the [US post-invasion mission in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Iraq_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)).[^cite_note-197] During the second Iraq War, Kazakhstani troops dismantled 4 million mines and other explosives, helped provide medical care to more than 5,000 coalition members and civilians, and purified 718 cubic metres (25,400 cu ft) of water.[^cite_note-198]

Kazakhstan's [National Security Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Committee_of_the_Republic_of_Kazakhstan) (UQK) was established on 13 June 1992. It includes the Service of Internal Security, Military Counterintelligence, Border Guard, several Commando units, and Foreign Intelligence (Barlau). The latter is considered the most important part of KNB. Its director is [Nurtai Abykayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurtai_Abykayev).

Since 2002 the joint tactical peacekeeping exercise "Steppe Eagle" has been hosted by the Kazakhstan government. "Steppe Eagle" focuses on building coalitions and gives participating nations the opportunity to work together. During the Steppe Eagle exercises, the [KAZBAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KAZBAT) peacekeeping battalion operates within a multinational force under a unified command within peacekeeping operations, with NATO and the US Military.[^cite_note-steppe1-199]

In December 2013 Kazakhstan announced it will send officers to support United Nations Peacekeeping forces in Haiti, Western Sahara, Ivory Coast and Liberia.[^cite_note-un1-200]

### Political issues

The main political issues in Kazakhstan were low personal incomes, inflation, corruption and unemployment according to a 2026 Kazakhstan Institute of Public Development poll.[^cite_note-i610-201]

A 2017 OECD report on Kazakhstan indicated that Kazakhstan has reformed laws with regard to the [civil service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_service_in_Kazakhstan), judiciary, instruments to prevent corruption, [access to information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Access_to_information), and prosecuting [corruption in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Kazakhstan).[^cite_note-oecdreport2017-202] Kazakhstan has implemented anticorruption reforms that have been recognized by organizations like [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International).[^cite_note-tpi-203]

In 2011 Switzerland confiscated US\$48 million in Kazakhstani assets from Swiss bank accounts, as a result of a bribery investigation in the United States.[^cite_note-basel-204] US officials believed the funds represented bribes paid by American officials to Kazakhstani officials in exchange for oil or prospecting rights in Kazakhstan. Proceedings eventually involved US\$84 million in the US and another US\$60 million in Switzerland.[^cite_note-basel-204]

The [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI) and the Kazakh Anti-Corruption Agency signed a Mutual Legal Assistance Treaty in February 2015.[^cite_note-MLATfeb15-205]

#### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Kazakhstan)

Kazakhstan was ranked 142nd out of 180 countries in [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md)' [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) for 2024; previously it ranked 134th for 2023.[^cite_note-206]

Kazakhstan's human rights situation has been described as poor by independent observers. In its 2015 report of human rights in the country, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) said that "Kazakhstan heavily restricts freedom of assembly, speech, and religion."[^cite_note-World_Report_2015:_Kazakhstan-207] It has also described the government as authoritarian.[^cite_note-208] In 2014, authorities closed newspapers, jailed or fined dozens of people after peaceful but unsanctioned protests, and fined or detained worshipers for practising religion outside state controls. Government critics, including opposition leader [Vladimir Kozlov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Kozlov_\(politician\)), remained in detention after unfair trials. In mid-2014, Kazakhstan adopted new criminal, criminal executive, criminal procedural, and administrative codes, and a new law on trade unions, which contain articles restricting fundamental freedoms and are incompatible with international standards. Torture remains common in places of detention."[^cite_note-209] However, Kazakhstan has achieved significant progress in reducing the prison population.[^cite_note-210] The 2016 Human Rights Watch report commented that Kazakhstan "took few meaningful steps to tackle a worsening human rights record in 2015, maintaining a focus on economic development over political reform."[^cite_note-211] Some critics of the government have been [arrested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation#Efforts_to_combat_misinformation) for allegedly spreading [false information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_information) about the [COVID-19 pandemic in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Kazakhstan).[^cite_note-212] Various police reforms, like creation of local police service and zero-tolerance policing, aimed at bringing police closer to local communities, have not improved cooperation between policemen and ordinary citizens.[^cite_note-213]

According to a US government report released in 2014, in Kazakhstan:

> The law does not require police to inform detainees that they have the [right to an attorney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_an_attorney), and police did not do so. Human rights observers alleged that law enforcement officials dissuaded detainees from seeing an attorney, gathered evidence through preliminary questioning before a detainee's attorney arrived, and in some cases used corrupt defense attorneys to gather evidence.[^cite_note-countryreport-214]
>
> \[...]The law does not provide for an [independent judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_judiciary). The executive branch sharply limited judicial independence. Prosecutors enjoyed a quasi-judicial role and had the authority to suspend court decisions.
>
> &#x20;Corruption was evident at every stage of the judicial process. Although judges were among the most highly paid government employees, lawyers and human rights monitors alleged that judges, prosecutors, and other officials solicited bribes in exchange for favorable rulings in the majority of criminal cases.[^cite_note-countryreport-214]

Kazakhstan's global rank in the [World Justice Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Justice_Project)'s 2015 Rule of Law Index was 65 out of 102; the country scored well on "Order and Security" (global rank 32/102), and poorly on "Constraints on Government Powers" (global rank 93/102), "Open Government" (85/102) and "Fundamental Rights" (84/102, with a downward trend marking a deterioration in conditions).[^cite_note-WJP2015-215]

The [ABA Rule of Law Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABA_Rule_of_Law_Initiative) of the American Bar Association has programs to train justice sector professionals in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-ABArol-216][^cite_note-ABAback-217]

Kazakhstan's Supreme Court has taken steps to modernise and to increase transparency and oversight over the country's legal system. With funding from the US Agency for International Development, the ABA Rule of Law Initiative began a new program in April 2012 to strengthen the independence and accountability of Kazakhstan's judiciary.[^cite_note-ABAjr-218]

In an effort to increase transparency in the criminal justice and court system, and improve human rights, Kazakhstan intended to digitize all investigative, prosecutorial and court records by 2018.[^cite_note-digitise-219] Many criminal cases are closed before trial on the basis of reconciliation between the defendant and the victim because they simplify the work of the law-enforcement officers, release the defendant from punishment, and pay little regard to the victim's rights.[^cite_note-220]

Homosexuality has been legal in Kazakhstan since 1997, although it is still socially unacceptable in most areas.[^cite_note-221] Discrimination against [LGBT people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Kazakhstan) in Kazakhstan is widespread.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Kazakhstan)



GDP per capita development in Kazakhstan since 1973



A multifunctional building and financial centre of the city of Almaty

In 2018, Kazakhstan had a GDP of \$179.332 billion and an annual growth rate of 4.5%. Per capita, Kazakhstan's GDP stood at \$9,686.[^cite_note-224] Buoyed by high world [crude oil](23195.md) prices, GDP growth figures were between 8.9% and 13.5% from 2000 to 2007 before decreasing to 1% to 3% during the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), and then rising again from 2010.[^cite_note-225] Other major exports of Kazakhstan include wheat, textiles, and livestock. Kazakhstan is a leading exporter of [uranium](31743.md).[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227]

Kazakhstan's economy grew by 4.6% in 2014.[^cite_note-BRICplus-228] The country experienced a slowdown in economic growth from 2014 sparked by a falling [price of oil](5137675.md) and the effects of the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md).[^cite_note-229] The country devalued its currency by 19% in February 2014.[^cite_note-230] Another 22% devaluation occurred in August 2015.[^cite_note-231] Kazakhstan was the first former Soviet Republic to repay all of its debt to the International Monetary Fund, 7 years ahead of schedule.[^cite_note-IMFrepay-232]

Kazakhstan weathered the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) by combining fiscal relaxation with monetary stabilization. In 2009, the government introduced large-scale support measures such as the recapitalization of banks and support for the real estate and agricultural sectors, as well as for [small and medium enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_and_medium_enterprise) (SMEs). The total value of the stimulus programs amounted to \$21 billion, or 20% of the country's GDP, with \$4 billion going to stabilize the financial sector.[^cite_note-ft21bn-233] During the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), Kazakhstan's economy contracted by 1.2% in 2009, but increased to 7.5% and 5% in 2011 and 2012, respectively.[^cite_note-imfarticleiv-234] Kazakhstan's government continued to follow a conservative fiscal policy by controlling budget spending and accumulating oil revenue savings in its Oil Fund – Samruk-Kazyna. The [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) forced Kazakhstan to increase its public borrowing to support the economy. Public debt increased to 13.4% in 2013 from 8.7% in 2008. Between 2012 and 2013, the government achieved an overall fiscal surplus of 4.5%.[^cite_note-kzwbprofile-235]

In March 2002 the [US Department of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Department_of_Commerce) granted Kazakhstan [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) status under [US trade law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_Act_of_2002). This change in status recognized substantive market economy reforms in the areas of currency [convertibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convertibility), wage rate determination, openness to foreign investment, and government control over the means of production and allocation of resources. In September 2002, Kazakhstan became the first country in the [CIS](36870.md) to receive an investment grade [credit rating](1194146.md) from a major international [credit rating agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_rating_agency).[^cite_note-236] By late December 2003, Kazakhstan's gross foreign debt was about \$22.9 billion. Total governmental debt was \$4.2 billion, 14% of GDP. There has been a reduction in the ratio of debt to GDP. The ratio of total governmental debt to GDP was 21.7% in 2000, 17.5% in 2001, and 15.4% in 2002. In 2019, it rose to 19.2%.[^cite_note-237]



[Aqtau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqtau) is Kazakhstan's only seaport on the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md).

On 29 November 2003 the Law on Changes to Tax Code, which reduced [tax rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_rates_around_the_world), was adopted. The [value added tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_added_tax) fell from 16% to 15%, the social tax, payable by all employers, from 21% to 20%, and the personal [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax) from 30% to 20%. On 7 July 2006, the personal income tax was reduced even further to a flat rate of 5% for personal income in the form of dividends and 10% for other personal income. Kazakhstan furthered its reforms by adopting a new land code on 20 June 2003 and a new customs code on 5 April 2003.

Kazakhstan instituted a pension reform program in 1998. By January 2012 the pension assets were about \$17 billion (KZT 2.5 trillion). There are 11 savings pension funds in the country. The State Accumulating Pension Fund, the only state-owned fund, was [privatized](24661.md) in 2006. The country's unified financial regulatory agency oversees and regulates pension funds. The growing demand of pension funds for investment outlets triggered the development of the debt [securities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_\(finance\)) market. Pension fund capital is being invested almost exclusively in corporate and government [bonds](60737.md), including the government of Kazakhstan Eurobonds. The government of Kazakhstan was studying a project to create a unified national pension fund and transfer all the accounts from the private pension funds into it.[^cite_note-238]

Kazakhstan climbed to 41st on the 2018 Economic Freedom Index published by *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* and [The Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation).[^cite_note-EFI2018-239]

### Foreign trade



A map of Kazakhstan's imports, 2013

Kazakhstan's increased role in global trade and central positioning on the [new Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative) gave the country the potential to open its markets to billions of people.[^cite_note-priSilkRoad-240] Kazakhstan joined the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) in 2015.[^cite_note-241]

Kazakhstan's foreign trade turnover in 2018 was \$93.5 billion, which is 19.7% more than in 2017. Exports in 2018 reached \$67 billion (up 25.7% in comparison to 2017) and imports were \$32.5 billion (up 9.9% in comparison to 2017).[^cite_note-242] Exports accounted for 40.1% of Kazakhstan's gross domestic product (GDP) in 2018. Kazakhstan exports 800 products to 120 countries.[^cite_note-243]

### Agriculture

Main article: [Agriculture in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Kazakhstan)



Grain fields near [Kökşetau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6k%C5%9Fetau)

[Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Kazakhstan) accounts for approximately 5% of Kazakhstan's GDP.[^cite_note-CIA-167] Grain, potatoes, grapes, vegetables, melons and livestock are the most important agricultural commodities. Agricultural land occupies more than 846,000 square kilometres (327,000 sq mi). The available agricultural land consists of 205,000 km<sup>2</sup> (79,000 sq mi) of arable land and 611,000 km<sup>2</sup> (236,000 sq mi) of [pasture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasture) and hay land. Over 80% of the country's total area is classified as agricultural land, including almost 70% occupied by pasture. Its arable land has the second highest availability per inhabitant (1.5 hectares).[^cite_note-wb1-244]

Chief livestock products are dairy products, leather, meat, and wool. The country's major crops include wheat, [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), cotton, and rice. Wheat exports, a major source of [hard currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_currency), rank among the leading commodities in Kazakhstan's export trade. In 2003, Kazakhstan harvested 17.6 million tons of grain in gross, 2.8% higher compared to 2002. Kazakhstani agriculture still has many environmental problems from mismanagement during its years in the Soviet Union. Some [Kazakh wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_wine) is produced in the mountains to the east of Almaty.[^cite_note-245]

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Kazakhstan)



Kazakhstan has one of the largest proven [oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Kazakhstan) in the Caspian Sea region.

Energy has been the leading economic sector. Production of crude oil and [natural gas condensate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_condensate) from the [oil and gas basins of Kazakhstan](18539150.md) amounted to 79.2 million tonnes (77.9 million [long tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_ton); 87.3 million [short tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_ton)) in 2012 up from 51.2 million tonnes (50.4 million long tons; 56.4 million short tons) in 2003. Kazakhstan raised oil and gas condensate exports to 44.3 million tons in 2003, 13% higher than in 2002. Gas production in Kazakhstan in 2003 amounted to 13.9 billion cubic metres (490 [billion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1000000000_\(number\)) [cubic feet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubic_foot)), up 22.7% compared to 2002, including natural gas production of 7.3 billion cubic metres (260 billion cubic feet). Kazakhstan holds about 4 billion tonnes (3.9 billion long tons; 4.4 billion short tons) of proven recoverable oil reserves and 2,000 cubic kilometres (480 cubic miles) of gas. Kazakhstan is the 19th largest oil-producing nation in the world.[^cite_note-246] Kazakhstan's oil exports in 2003, were valued at more than \$7 billion, representing 65% of overall exports and 24% of the GDP. Major oil and gas fields and recoverable [oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_reserves) are [Tengiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengiz_Field) with 7 billion barrels (1.1 billion cubic metres); [Karachaganak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachaganak_Field) with 8 billion barrels (1.3 billion cubic metres) and 1,350 cubic kilometres (320 cubic miles) of natural gas; and [Kashagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashagan_Field) with 7 to 9 billion barrels (1.4 billion cubic metres).

[KazMunayGas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KazMunayGas) (KMG), the national oil and gas company, was created in 2002 to represent the interests of the state in the oil and gas industry. The [Tengiz Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengiz_Field) was jointly developed in 1993 as a 40-year [Tengizchevroil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengizchevroil) venture between [Chevron Texaco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_Texaco) (50%), US [ExxonMobil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ExxonMobil) (25%), [KazMunayGas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KazMunayGas) (20%), and [LukArco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LukArco) (5%).[^cite_note-247] The [Karachaganak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachaganak_Field) natural gas and gas condensate field is being developed by [BG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BG_Group), [Agip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agip), ChevronTexaco, and [Lukoil](1027881.md).[^cite_note-ogj280612-248] Also Chinese oil companies are involved in Kazakhstan's oil industry.[^cite_note-249]

Kazakhstan launched the Green Economy Plan in 2013. It committed Kazakhstan to meet 50% of its energy needs from alternative and renewable sources by 2050.[^cite_note-250] The green economy was projected to increase GDP by 3% and create some 500,000 jobs.[^cite_note-251] The government set prices for energy produced from renewable sources. The price of 1 kilowatt-hour for energy produced by wind power plants was set at 22.68 tenge (\$0.12), for 1 kilowatt-hour produced by small hydro-power plants 16.71 tenges (\$0.09), and from biogas plants 32.23 tenges (\$0.18).[^cite_note-renewableprices-252]

### Infrastructure

Main articles: [Transport in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Kazakhstan) and [Telecommunications in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Kazakhstan)



Kazakhstan's railway network



Train 22 Kyzylorda – Semipalatinsk, hauled by a Kazakhstan Temir Zholy 2TE10U diesel locomotive. Picture taken near Aynabulak, Kazakhstan.

Railways provide 68% of all cargo and passenger traffic to over 57% of the country. There are 15,333 km (9,527 mi) in common carrier service, excluding industrial lines.[^cite_note-ciafactbook-253] 15,333 km (9,527 mi) of [1,520 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5_ft_and_1520_mm_gauge_railways) (4 ft 11+27⁄32 in) gauge, 4,000 km (2,500 mi) electrified, in 2012.[^cite_note-ciafactbook-253] Most cities are connected by railroad; high-speed trains go from [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) (the southernmost city) to [Petropavl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petropavl) (the northernmost city) in about 18 hours.

[Kazakhstan Temir Zholy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_Temir_Zholy) (KTZ) is the national railway company. KTZ cooperates with French locomotive manufacturer [Alstom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alstom) in developing Kazakhstan's railway infrastructure. As of 2018, Alstom has more than 600 staff and two joint ventures with KTZ and its subsidiary in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-254] In July 2017, Alstom opened its first locomotive repairing centre in Kazakhstan. It is the only repairing centre in Central Asia and the Caucasus.[^cite_note-255] [Astana Nurly Zhol railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana_Nurly_Zhol_railway_station), the most modern railway station in Kazakhstan, was opened in Astana on 31 May 2017. According to Kazakhstan Railways (KTZ), the 120,000 m<sup>2</sup> (1,300,000 ft<sup>2</sup>) station was expected to be used by 54 trains and would have the capacity to handle 35,000 passengers a day.[^cite_note-256]

There is a small 8.56 km (5.32 mi) [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) system in [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty). Second and third metro lines were planned for the future. The second line would intersect with the first line at [Alatau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alatau_\(Almaty_Metro\)) and [Zhibek Zholy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhibek_Zholy_\(Almaty_Metro\)) stations.[^cite_note-urbanrail.net-257] The [Astana Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana_Metro) system has been under construction, but was abandoned at one point in 2013.[^cite_note-258] In May 2015, an agreement was signed for the project to be resumed.[^cite_note-259] There is an 86 km (53 mi) tram network, which began service in 1965 with, as of 2012, 20 regular and three special routes.[^cite_note-Pavlodar-260]

The [Khorgos Gateway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorgos_Gateway) dry port is one of Kazakhstan's primary [dry ports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_port) for handling trans-Eurasian trains, which travel more than 9,000 km (5,600 mi) between China and Europe. The Khorgos Gateway dry port is surrounded by Khorgos Eastern Gate SEZ, which officially commenced operations in December 2016.[^cite_note-261]

In 2009 the [European Commission](9974.md) blacklisted all Kazakh air carriers with a sole exception of [Air Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Astana).[^cite_note-262] Thereafter, Kazakhstan took measures to modernise and revamp its air safety oversight. In 2016 the European air safety authorities removed all Kazakh airlines from the blacklist, saying there was "sufficient evidence of compliance" with international standards by Kazakh Airlines and the Civil Aviation Committee.[^cite_note-263]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Kazakhstan)



[Lake Burabay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Burabay), view from Mount Bolectau



[Shymbulak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shymbulak) ski resort in Almaty



The Nur Alem Pavilion at EXPO 2017 in [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana)



[Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty)

Kazakhstan is the [ninth-largest country by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) and the largest landlocked country in the world. As of 2014 tourism accounted for 0.3% of Kazakhstan's GDP, but the government had plans to increase it to 3% by 2020.[^cite_note-theguardian1-264][^cite_note-265] According to the [World Economic Forum](172185.md)'s [Travel and Tourism Competitiveness Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_and_Tourism_Competitiveness_Report) of 2017, travel and tourism industry GDP in Kazakhstan was \$3.08 billion or only 1.6% of total GDP. The WEF ranked Kazakhstan 80th in its 2019 report.[^cite_note-266]

In 2017 Kazakhstan ranked 43rd in the number of tourist arrivals. In 2014 *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* described tourism in Kazakhstan as "hugely underdeveloped," despite the country's mountain, lake, and desert landscapes.[^cite_note-Lillis-267] Factors hampering an increase in tourism were said to include high prices, "shabby infrastructure," "poor service," and the difficulties of travel in a large underdeveloped country.[^cite_note-Lillis-267] Even for Kazakhs, going for a holiday abroad may cost only half the price of taking a holiday in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-Lillis-267]

The Kazakh government, long characterized as authoritarian with a history of human rights abuses and suppression of political opposition,[^cite_note-time-24] in 2015 issued a "Tourism Industry Development Plan 2020." It aimed to establish five tourism clusters in Kazakhstan: [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana) city, [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) city, [East Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Kazakhstan_Region), [South Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Kazakhstan_Region), and [West Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Kazakhstan_Region) Oblasts. It also sought investment of \$4 billion and the creation of 300,000 new jobs in the tourism industry by 2020.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-Lillis-267]

Kazakhstan has offered a permanent visa-free regime for up to 90 days to citizens of [Armenia](10918072.md), [Azerbaijan](746.md), [Belarus](3457.md), Georgia, [Moldova](19260.md), [Kyrgyzstan](170131.md), [Mongolia](19271.md), Russia and [Ukraine](31750.md), and for up to 30 days to citizens of [Argentina](18951905.md), Brazil, [Ecuador](9334.md), [Serbia](29265.md), [South Korea](27019.md), [Tajikistan](30108.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [UAE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UAE) and [Uzbekistan](31853.md). It also established a visa-free regime for citizens of 54 countries, including the European Union and [OECD member states](33853117.md#Member_countries), the US, Japan, Mexico, Australia and New Zealand.[^cite_note-tim-269][^cite_note-mfa.kz-270]

### Foreign direct investment

Kazakhstan has attracted \$330 billion in foreign direct investment (FDI) from more than 120 countries since its independence (1991).[^cite_note-271] In 2015, the US State Department said Kazakhstan was widely considered to have the best investment climate in the region.[^cite_note-2013ICS-272] In 2014, President Nazarbayev signed into law tax concessions to promote foreign direct investment which included a 10-year exemption from corporation tax, an eight-year exemption from property tax, and a 10-year freeze on most other taxes.[^cite_note-taxnews-273] Other incentives include a refund on capital investments of up to 30% once a production facility is in operation.[^cite_note-taxnews-273] In 2012, Kazakhstan attracted \$14 billion of [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) inflows into the country at a 7% growth rate.[^cite_note-274] In 2018, \$24 billion of FDI was directed into Kazakhstan, a significant increase since 2012.[^cite_note-275]

In 2014 the [European Bank of Reconstruction and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Bank_of_Reconstruction_and_Development) (EBRD) and Kazakhstan created the partnership for Re-Energizing the Reform Process in Kazakhstan to work with international financial institutions to channel US\$2.7 billion provided by the Kazakh government into important sectors of Kazakhstan's economy.[^cite_note-EBRDprrp-276] As of May 2014 Kazakhstan had attracted \$190 billion in gross foreign investments since its independence in 1991 and it led the CIS countries in terms of FDI attracted per capita.[^cite_note-AstanaT-277] The OECD 2017 Investment Policy Review noted that "great strides" had been made to open up opportunities to foreign investors and improve policy to attract FDI.[^cite_note-oecdkzpolicyreview-278] China is one of the main economic and trade partners of Kazakhstan. In 2013, China launched the [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative) (BRI) in which Kazakhstan functions as a transit hub.[^cite_note-279]

### Banking

The banking sector in Kazakhstan experienced a boom-and-bust cycle in the early 21st century. Following a period of rapid growth during the mid-2000s, the industry collapsed in 2008 due to the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis). Several major banks, including [BTA Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BTA_Bank) J.S.C. and Alliance Bank, defaulted shortly thereafter. In the aftermath, the sector underwent significant contraction and restructuring, with total system-wide loans declining from 59% of GDP in 2007 to 39% by 2011. To stabilize the sector, the [National Bank of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bank_of_Kazakhstan) introduced a [deposit insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deposit_insurance) scheme as part of broader reforms. During this period, several international banks operated in Kazakhstan, including [RBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Bank_of_Scotland), [Citibank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citibank), and [HSBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HSBC). Additionally, foreign institutions such as South Korea's [Kookmin Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kookmin_Bank) and Italy's [UniCredit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UniCredit) entered the Kazakh market through [acquisitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acquisitions) and [equity investments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equity_investment).

### Economic competitiveness

According to the 2010–11 World Economic Forum in Global Competitiveness Report, Kazakhstan was ranked 72nd in the world in economic competitiveness.[^cite_note-280] One year later, the [Global Competitiveness Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Competitiveness_Report) ranked Kazakhstan 50th in most competitive markets.[^cite_note-281]

In the 2020 Doing Business Report by the World Bank, Kazakhstan ranked 25th globally and as the number one best country globally for protecting minority investors' rights.[^cite_note-282] Kazakhstan achieved its goal of entering the top 50 most competitive countries in 2013 and has maintained its position in the 2014–2015 World Economic Forum [Global Competitiveness Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Competitiveness_Report) that was published at the beginning of September 2014.[^cite_note-AT1-283] Kazakhstan is ahead of other states in the CIS in almost all of the report's pillars of competitiveness, including institutions, infrastructure, macroeconomic environment, higher education and training, goods market efficiency, labour market development, financial market development, technological readiness, market size, business sophistication and innovation, lagging behind only in the category of health and primary education.[^cite_note-AT1-283] The Global Competitiveness Index gives a score from 1 to 7 in each of these pillars, and Kazakhstan earned an overall score of 4.4.[^cite_note-AT1-283]

### Science and technology

Main article: [Science and technology in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Kazakhstan)



Trends in research expenditure in Central Asia, as a percentage of GDP, 2001–2013. Source: UNESCO Science Report: 2030 (2015), figure 14.3.

Research remains largely concentrated in Kazakhstan's largest city and former capital, Almaty, home to 52% of research personnel. Public research is largely confined to institutes, with universities making only a token contribution. Research institutes receive their funding from national research councils under the umbrella of the Ministry of Education and Science. Their output, however, tends to be disconnected from market needs. In the business sector, few industrial enterprises conduct research themselves.[^cite_note-\:02-284][^cite_note-\:1-285]



Group of Kazakh physicists in collaboration with Uzbek researchers working at the ion accelerator DC-60

One of the most ambitious targets of the State Programme for Accelerated Industrial and Innovative Development, adopted in 2010, is to raise the country's level of expenditure on research and development to 1% of GDP by 2015. By 2013, this ratio stood at 0.18% of GDP. It will be difficult to reach the target as long as economic growth remains strong. Since 2005, the economy has grown faster (by 6% in 2013) than gross domestic expenditure on research and development, which only progressed from PPP\$598 million to PPP\$714 million between 2005 and 2013.[^cite_note-\:1-285]

Innovation expenditure more than doubled in Kazakhstan between 2010 and 2011, representing KZT 235 billion (c. US\$1.6 billion), or around 1.1% of GDP. Some 11% of the total was spent on research and development. This compares with about 40 to 70% of innovation expenditure in developed countries. This augmentation was due to a sharp rise in product design and the introduction of new services and production methods over this period, to the detriment of the acquisition of machinery and equipment, which has traditionally made up the bulk of Kazakhstan's innovation expenditure. Training costs represented just 2% of innovation expenditure, a much lower share than in developed countries.[^cite_note-\:02-284][^cite_note-\:1-285] Kazakhstan was ranked 81st in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287]

In December 2012 President [Nursultan Nazarbayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursultan_Nazarbayev) announced the *Kazakhstan 2050 Strategy* with the slogan "Strong Business, Strong State." This pragmatic strategy proposes sweeping socio-economic and political reforms to hoist Kazakhstan among the top 30 economies by 2050. In this document, Kazakhstan gives itself 15 years to evolve into a knowledge economy. New sectors are to be created during each five-year plan. The first of these, covering the years 2010–2014, focused on developing industrial capacity in car manufacturing, aircraft engineering and the production of locomotives, passenger and cargo railroad cars. During the second five-year plan to 2019, the goal is to develop export markets for these products. To enable Kazakhstan to enter the world market of geological exploration, the country intends to increase the efficiency of traditional extractive sectors such as oil and gas. It also intends to develop rare earth metals, given their importance for electronics, laser technology, communication and medical equipment. The second five-year plan coincides with the development of the *Business 2020* roadmap for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), which makes provision for the allocation of grants to SMEs in the regions and for microcredit. The government and the National Chamber of Entrepreneurs also plan to develop an effective mechanism to help start-ups.[^cite_note-\:1-285]



[Baikonur Cosmodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baikonur_Cosmodrome) is the world's oldest and largest operational [spaceport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceport).

During the subsequent five-year plans to 2050, new industries will be established in fields such as mobile, multimedia, nano- and space technologies, robotics, genetic engineering and alternative energy. Food processing enterprises will be developed with an eye to turning the country into a major regional exporter of beef, dairy, and other agricultural products. Low-return, water-intensive crop varieties will be replaced with vegetable, oil and fodder products. As part of the shift to a "green economy" by 2030, 15% of acreage will be cultivated with water-saving technologies. Experimental agrarian and innovational clusters will be established and drought-resistant [genetically modified crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_crops) developed.[^cite_note-\:1-285]

The Kazakhstan 2050 Strategy fixes a target of devoting 3% of GDP to research and development by 2050 to allow for the development of new high-tech sectors.[^cite_note-\:1-285]

The Digital Kazakhstan program was launched in 2018 to boost the country's economic growth through the implementation of digital technologies. Kazakhstan's digitization efforts generated 800 billion tenges (US\$1.97 billion) in two years. The program helped create 120,000 jobs and attracted 32.8 billion tenges (US\$80.7 million) of investment into the country.

Around 82% of all public services became automated as part of the Digital Kazakhstan program.[^cite_note-288]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Kazakhstan](16645.md)

See also: [Kazakhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs)



[Population pyramid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_pyramid), 2023

Kazakhstan had an estimated population of 20.8 million as of 2025.[^cite_note-\:2-289] According to official statistics, the country's population surpassed 20 million in September 2024,[^cite_note-290] compared to 17,280,000 a decade earlier.[^cite_note-291] This reflects significant growth since independence in 1991, when the population was 16.4 million, before subsequently declining to 14.8 million by 2001, due mostly to emigration by ethnic Russians.[^cite_note-292]

In contrast to most [post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states), Kazakhstan has maintained [replacement rate fertility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replacement_rate) since independence, with a [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) of 2.53 in 2025.[^cite_note-\:2-289] Consequently, the country has a young population, with 29% of Kazakhstanis being 14 years old and younger, and only 9% aged 65 and older.[^cite_note-\:2-289]

In the 2024 Global Hunger Index (GHI), Kazakhstan ranks 25th out of 127 countries with sufficient data. Kazakhstan's GHI score is 5.3, which indicates a low level of hunger.[^cite_note-293]
