### Ethnic groups

Main article: [Ethnic demography of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_demography_of_Kazakhstan)



Central Asian ethnolinguistic patchwork, 1992

As of 2026 ethnic [Kazakhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs) are 71.5% of the population and ethnic [Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Kazakhstan) are 14.4%. The ethnic Russian population has significantly declined since the [breakup of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-295] Other groups include [Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars) (1.1%), [Ukrainians](145144.md) (1.8%), [Uzbeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbeks) (3.4%), [Germans](152735.md) (1.1%), [Uyghurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_people) (1.5%), [Azerbaijanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijanis), [Dungans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dungans), [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples), [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans), [Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poles_in_the_former_Soviet_Union), and [Lithuanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanians). Some minorities such as [Ukrainians](145144.md), [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans), [Volga Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Germans), [Chechens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chechens),[^cite_note-296] [Meskhetian Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meskhetian_Turks), and Russian political opponents of the regime, had been [deported to Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union) in the 1930s and 1940s by Josef Stalin. Some of the largest Soviet [labour camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_camp) ([Gulag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag)) existed in the country.[^cite_note-297]

Significant Russian immigration was also connected with the [Virgin Lands Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Lands_Campaign) and [Soviet space program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_space_program) during the [Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) era.[^cite_note-298] In 1989, ethnic Russians were 37.8% of the population and Kazakhs held a majority in only 7 of the 20 regions of the country. Before 1991 there were about one million [Germans in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germans_of_Kazakhstan), mostly descendants of the [Volga Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Germans) deported to Kazakhstan during World War II. After the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), most of them emigrated to Germany.[^cite_note-299] Most members of the smaller [Pontian Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontian_Greek) minority have emigrated to Greece. In the late 1930s thousands of [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans) in the Soviet Union were [deported to Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_Koreans_in_the_Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-300] These people are now known as [Koryo-saram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koryo-saram).[^cite_note-301]

The 1990s were marked by the emigration of many of the country's [Russians](59510.md), [Ukrainians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainians_in_Kazakhstan) and [Volga Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Germans), a process that began in the 1970s. This has made indigenous Kazakhs the largest ethnic group.[^cite_note-302] Additional factors in the increase in the Kazakhstani population are higher birthrates and [immigration of ethnic Kazakhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oralman) from China, [Mongolia](19271.md), and Russia.

As of 2026, 63.6% of the population lives in urban areas.[^cite_note-303]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Kazakhstan)

Kazakhstan is officially a bilingual country.[^cite_note-304] [Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language) (part of the [Kipchak sub-branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kipchak_languages) of the [Turkic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages))[^cite_note-305] was proficiently spoken by 80.1% of the population according to 2021 census,[^cite_note-census-306]<sup>: 323 </sup> and has the status of "state language". Russian, on the other hand, was spoken by 83.7% as of 2021.[^cite_note-307] It has equal status to Kazakh as an "official language", and is used routinely in business, government, and inter-ethnic communication.[^cite_note-308] However, only 63.4% of ethnic Kazakhs and 49.3% of the country's population are daily speakers of the Kazakh language, according to the same census.[^cite_note-census-306]<sup>: 382 </sup>

The government announced in January 2015 that the [Latin alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_alphabet) would replace [Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic) as the writing system for the Kazakh language by 2025.[^cite_note-inform.kz-309] Other minority languages spoken in Kazakhstan include [Uzbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbek_language), [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language), [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_language), [Kyrgyz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrgyz_language), [Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatar_language), and German. English, as well as Turkish, have gained popularity among younger people since the collapse of the Soviet Union. Education across Kazakhstan is conducted in either Kazakh, Russian, or both.[^cite_note-310] In Nazarbayev's resignation speech of 2019, he projected that the people of Kazakhstan in the future will speak three languages (Kazakh, Russian and English).[^cite_note-311]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Kazakhstan)

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Kazakhstan) |  | 69.3% |
| [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Kazakhstan) |  | 17.2% |
| No response |  | 11.01% |
| [Atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Kazakhstan) |  | 2.25% |
| [Other religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Kazakhstan) |  | 0.2% |

According to the 2021 census, 69.3% of the population is [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Kazakhstan), 17.2% are [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Kazakhstan), 0.2% follow [other religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Kazakhstan) (mostly [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Kazakhstan) and [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Kazakhstan)), 11.01% chose not to answer, and 2.25% identify as [atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheist).[^cite_note-2021EthnicStatistics-5][^cite_note-Viktorova-6]

Kazakhstan is a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) whose constitution guarantees religious freedoms. Article 39 of the constitution states: "Human rights and freedoms shall not be restricted in any way." Article 14 prohibits "discrimination on religious basis" and Article 19 ensures that everyone has the "right to determine and indicate or not to indicate his/her ethnic, party and religious affiliation." The Constitutional Council affirmed these rights in a 2009 declaration, which stated that a proposed law limiting the rights of certain individuals to practice their religion was declared unconstitutional.[^cite_note-312]

[Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Kazakhstan) is the largest religion in Kazakhstan, followed by [Eastern Orthodox Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Kazakhstan). After decades of [religious suppression by the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_Soviet_Union), the coming of independence witnessed a surge in the expression of ethnic identity, partly through religion. The free practice of [religious beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) and the establishment of full freedom of religion led to an increase in religious activity. Hundreds of mosques, churches, and other religious structures were built in the span of a few years, with the number of religious associations rising from 670 in 1990 to 4,170 today.[^cite_note-kazakhrel-313]

Some figures show that [non-denominational Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslims)[^cite_note-314] form the majority, while others indicate that most Muslims in the country are [Sunnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) following the [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi) school.[^cite_note-315] These include ethnic [Kazakhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhs), who constitute about 7% of the population, as well as ethnic [Uzbeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbeks), [Uighurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uighurs), and [Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars).[^cite_note-usstate-316] Less than 1% are part of the Sunni [Shafi\`i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi%60i) school (primarily [Chechens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chechens)). There are also some [Ahmadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya) Muslims.[^cite_note-317] There are a total of 2,300 mosques,[^cite_note-kazakhrel-313] all of them are affiliated with the "Spiritual Association of Muslims of Kazakhstan", headed by a supreme [mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti), who since 2020 is [Nauryzbay Taganuly Otpenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nauryzbay_Taganuly_Otpenov?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] Unaffiliated mosques are forcefully closed.[^cite_note-320] [Eid al-Adha](358226.md) is recognized as a national holiday.[^cite_note-kazakhrel-313] One quarter of the population is Russian Orthodox, including ethnic Russians, Ukrainians and Belarusians.[^cite_note-USCOIRFk2009-321] Other Christian groups include [Roman Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Kazakhstan), [Greek Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Catholics), and [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants).[^cite_note-usstate-316] There are a total of 258 Orthodox churches, 93 Catholic churches (9 [Greek Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Catholic)), and over 500 Protestant churches and prayer houses. The Russian Orthodox Christmas is recognized as a national holiday in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-kazakhrel-313] Other religious groups include Judaism, the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith_in_Kazakhstan), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Kazakhstan), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Central_Asia), and [the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints).[^cite_note-usstate-316]

According to the 2009 Census data, there are few Christians outside the Russian and Germanic ethnic groups.[^cite_note-322]

- The Astana Grand Mosque in the capital Astana. Islam is the majority religion in the country.

  The [Astana Grand Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana_Grand_Mosque) in the capital Astana. Islam is the majority religion in the country.

- Ascension Cathedral in Almaty

  [Ascension Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_Cathedral,_Almaty) in Almaty

- Cathedral of Our Lady of Fatima is the biggest Catholic church in Central Asia.

  [Cathedral of Our Lady of Fatima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Our_Lady_of_Fatima,_Karaganda) is the biggest Catholic church in Central Asia.

### Education

Main article: [Education in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Kazakhstan)



Kazakh National University of Arts

Education is universal and mandatory through to the [secondary level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education) and the [adult literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_literacy_rate) is 99.5%.[^cite_note-collegeatlas-323] On average, these statistics are equal for both women and men in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-324]

Education consists of three main phases: primary education (forms 1–4), basic general education (forms 5–9) and senior level education (forms 10–11 or 12), divided into continued general education and vocational education. Vocational Education usually lasts three or four years.[^cite_note-UNEVOC-325] (Primary education is preceded by one year of pre-school education.) These levels can be followed in one institution or in different ones (e.g., primary school, then secondary school). Recently, several secondary schools, specialized schools, magnet schools, [gymnasiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnasium_\(school\)), [lyceums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum#Lyceums_in_today's_education) and linguistic and technical gymnasiums have been founded. Secondary professional education is offered in special professional or technical schools, lyceums or colleges and vocational schools.[^cite_note-collegeatlas-323]

At present, there are [universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Kazakhstan), [academies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy) and institutes, [conservatories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_or_university_school_of_music), higher schools and higher colleges. There are three main levels: basic higher education that provides the fundamentals of the chosen field of study and leads to the award of the bachelor's degree; specialized higher education after which students are awarded the Specialist's Diploma; and scientific-pedagogical higher education, which leads to the master's degree. Postgraduate education leads to the *[Kandidat Nauk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandidat_Nauk)* ("Candidate of Sciences") and the Doctor of Sciences (PhD). With the adoption of the Laws on Education and on Higher Education, a private sector has been established and several private institutions have been licensed.

Over 2,500 students in Kazakhstan have applied for student loans totalling about \$9 million. The largest number of student loans comes from Almaty, Astana and Kyzylorda.[^cite_note-326]

The training and skills development programs in Kazakhstan are also supported by international organizations. For example, on 30 March 2015, the World Banks' Group of Executive Directors approved a \$100 million loan for the Skills and Job project in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-WB11-327] The project aims to provide training to unemployed, unproductively self-employed, and employees in need of training.[^cite_note-WB11-327]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Kazakhstan)

See also: [Kazakh clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_clothing) and [Kazakh wedding ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_wedding_ceremony)



A Kazakhstani performer demonstrates the long equestrian heritage as part of the gala concert during the opening ceremonies of the Central Asian Peacekeeping Battalion.

Before the Russian colonization, the Kazakhs had a highly developed culture based on their nomadic pastoral economy. [Islam](6037917.md) was introduced into the region with the arrival of the [Arabs](2185.md) in the 8th century. It initially took hold in the southern parts of [Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkestan) and spread northward.[^cite_note-328] The [Samanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanids) helped the religion take root through zealous missionary work. The [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) further propagated Islam amongst the tribes in the region during the 14th century.[^cite_note-329]

Kazakhstan is home to a large number of prominent contributors to literature, science and philosophy: [Abay Qunanbayuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abay_Qunanbayuli), [Mukhtar Auezov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhtar_Auezov), [Gabit Musirepov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabit_Musirepov), [Kanysh Satpayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanysh_Satpayev), [Mukhtar Shakhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhtar_Shakhanov), [Saken Seyfullin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saken_Seyfullin), [Jambyl Jabayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jambyl_Jabayev), among many others.

Tourism is a rapidly growing industry in Kazakhstan and it is joining the international tourism network. In 2010, Kazakhstan joined The Region Initiative (TRI), which is a Tri-regional Umbrella of Tourism-related organizations. TRI is functioning as a link between three regions: South Asia, Central Asia, and Eastern Europe. Armenia, Bangladesh, Georgia, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, India, Nepal, Pakistan, Russia, Sri Lanka, Tajikistan, Turkey, and Ukraine are now partners, and Kazakhstan is linked with other South Asian, Eastern European, and Central Asian countries in the tourism market.

### Fine arts

Main article: [Kazakh art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_art)

In Kazakhstan, the fine arts, in the classical sense, have their origins in the second half of the 19th century and the beginning of the 20th century. It was largely influenced by Russian artists, such as Vasily Vereshchagin and Nikolai Khludov, who traveled intensively in Central Asia. Khludov had a particular influence on the development of the local school of painting, becoming the teacher of many local artists. The most famous of these is Abilkhan Kasteyev, after whom the State Museum of Art of Kazakhstan was renamed in 1984.[^cite_note-330]

The Kazakh school of fine arts was fully formed by the 1940s and flourished in the 1950s. Local painters, graphic artists and sculptors, trained under the unified Soviet system of artist education, began active work, often using national motifs in their art. The painters O. Tansykbaev, J. Shardenov, K. Telzhanov, and S. Aitbaev, graphic artists E. Sidorkina and A. Duzelkhanov, and sculptors H. Nauryzbaeva and E. Sergebaeva are today counted among the key figures of Kazakhstani art.

### Literature

Main article: [Kazakh literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_literature)

Kazakh literature is defined as "the body of literature, both [oral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_literature) and written, produced in the [Kazakh language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language) by the [Kazakh people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_people) of Central Asia".[^cite_note-Feldman-331] Kazakh literature expands from the modern territory of Kazakhstan, including the era of [Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic), Kazakh recognized territory under the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and the [Kazakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate). There is some overlap with several complementary themes, including the literature of Turkic tribes that inhabited Kazakhstan over the course of its history and literature written by ethnic Kazakhs.



1965 Soviet stamp honouring Kazakh essayist and poet [Abai Qunanbaiuly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abai_Qunanbaiuly)

According to Chinese written sources from the 6th–8th centuries CE, the Turkic tribes of Kazakhstan had an oral poetry tradition. These came from earlier periods and were primarily transmitted by [bards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bard): professional storytellers and musical performers.[^cite_note-Nurmanova-332] Traces of this tradition are shown on [Orkhon script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Turkic_alphabet) stone carvings dated 5th–7th centuries CE that describe rule of Kultegin and Bilge, two early Turkic rulers ("kagans"). Amongst the Kazakhs, the bard was a primarily, though not exclusively, male profession. Since at least the 17th century, Kazakh bards could be divided into two main categories: the jıraws (jiraus, jyraus), who passed on the works of others, usually not creating and adding their own original work; and the [aqyns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqyn) (akyns), who improvised or created their own poems, stories or songs.[^cite_note-Feldman-331] There were several types of works, such as [didactic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didactic) *termes*, [elegiac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elegiac) *tolgaws*, and [epic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_\(genre\)) *jırs*.[^cite_note-Feldman-331] Although the origins of such tales are often unknown, most of them were associated with bards of the recent or more distant past, who supposedly created them or passed them on, by the time most Kazakh poetry and prose was first written down in the second half of the 19th century.[^cite_note-Feldman-331] There are clear stylistic differences between works first created in the 19th century, and works dating from earlier periods but not documented before the 19th century, such as those attributed to such 16th- and 17th-century bards as Er Shoban and Dosmombet Jıraw (also known as Dospambet Jyrau; he appeared to have been literate, and reportedly visited [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople)), and even to such 15th-century bards as Shalkiz and Asan Qayghı.[^cite_note-Feldman-331]

Other notable bards include Kaztugan Jyrau, Jiembet Jyrau, Axtamberdy Jyrau, and Buxar Jyrau Kalkamanuly, who was an advisor to [Ablai Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ablai_Khan), and whose works have been preserved by Mäšhür Jüsip Köpeev.[^cite_note-Nurmanova-332] *Er Targhın* and *[Alpamıs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpamysh)* are two of the most famous examples of Kazakh literature to be recorded in the 19th century.[^cite_note-Feldman-331] The *[Book of Dede Korkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Dede_Korkut)* and Oguz Name (a story of an ancient Turkic king [Oghuz Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Khan)) are the most well-known Turkic heroic legends. Initially created around the 9th century CE, they were passed on through generations in oral form. The legendary tales were recorded by Turkish authors in 14–16th centuries C.E.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334]

The preeminent role in the development of modern literary Kazakh belongs to [Abai Qunanbaiuly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abai_Qunanbaiuly) ([Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language): Абай Құнанбайұлы, sometimes Russified to Abay Kunanbayev, Абай Кунанбаев) (1845–1904), whose writings did much to preserve Kazakh folk culture. Abai's major work is *The Book of Words* ([Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language): қара сөздері, Qara sözderi), a philosophical treatise and collection of poems where he criticizes Russian colonial policies and encourages other Kazakhs to embrace education and literacy. The literary magazines *[Ay Qap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ay_Qap)* (published between 1911 and 1915 in Arabic script) and *Qazaq* (published between 1913 and 1918) played an important role in the development of the intellectual and political life among early 20th-century Kazakhs.[^cite_note-335]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Kazakhstan)



[Nowruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz) on stamp of Kazakhstan

The modern state of Kazakhstan is home to the Kazakh State Kurmangazy Orchestra of Folk Instruments, the Kazakh State Philharmonic Orchestra, the Kazakh National Opera and the Kazakh State Chamber Orchestra. The folk instrument orchestra was named after [Kurmangazy Sagyrbayuly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurmangazy_Sagyrbayuly), a famous composer and dombra player from the 19th century. The Musical-Dramatic Training College, founded in 1931, was the first institute of higher education for music. Two years later, the Orchestra of Kazakh Folk Musical Instruments was formed.[^cite_note-336] The Foundation Asyl Mura is archiving and publishing historical recordings of great samples of Kazakh music both traditional and classical. The leading conservatoire is in Almaty, the Qurmanghazy Conservatoire. It competes with the national conservatoire in Astana, Kazakhstan's capital.

When referring to traditional Kazakh music, authentic folklore must be separated from "folklorism". The latter denotes music executed by academically trained performers who aim at preserving the traditional music for future generations. As far as can be reconstructed, the music of Kazakhstan from the period before a strong Russian influence consists of instrumental music and vocal music. Instrumental music, with the pieces ("Küy") being performed by soloists. Text is often seen in the background (or "program") for the music, as many Küy titles refer to stories. Vocal music, either as part of a ceremony such as a wedding (mainly performed by women), or as part of a feast. Here we might divide into subgenres: epic singing, containing not only historical facts, but also the tribe's genealogy, love songs, and didactic verses; and, as a special form, the composition of two or more singers in public (Aitys), of dialogue character and usually unexpectedly frank in content.



[A-Studio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A-Studio) was created in 1982 in [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty), then called Alma-Ata, hence called "Alma-Ata Studio".

Russian influence on music in Kazakhstan developed in two main ways: through the introduction of academic institutions such as concert halls, opera stages, and conservatories, and through the incorporation of Kazakh traditional music into these structures. Under the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and later the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), Kazakh folk and classical traditions became increasingly connected with [ethnic Russian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_Russian_music) and Western European music. From the 19th century, Kazakh folk music was collected and studied by [ethnographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnography) researchers and transcribed into linear [notation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_notation), while some composers adapted Kazakh folk songs to Russian-style [European classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_classical_music). Kazakh musicians did not begin writing their own music in notation until 1931. Under the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), Kazakh folk culture was promoted in a controlled form, incorporating traditional music into Soviet musical institutions. In 1920, [Aleksandr Zatayevich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Zatayevich) created art music based on Kazakh folk melodies, while from 1928 he adapted traditional instruments for Russian-style ensembles by increasing their number of [frets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fret) and [strings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strings_\(music\)). By the 1930s, these orchestral styles had become the official form of Kazakh musical performance, transforming folk music into a patriotic and socialist cultural practice.[^cite_note-337]

### Film

Main article: [Cinema of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Kazakhstan)



[International Astana Action Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astana_Action_Film_Festival), 2010

Kazakhstan's film industry is run through the state-owned [Kazakhfilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhfilm) studios based in Almaty. The studio has produced movies such as *[Myn Bala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myn_Bala)*, *[Harmony Lessons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmony_Lessons)*, and *Shal*.[^cite_note-338] Kazakhstan is the host of the [International Astana Action Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astana_Action_Film_Festival) and the [Eurasia International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia_International_Film_Festival) held annually. Hollywood director [Timur Bekmambetov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur_Bekmambetov) is from Kazakhstan and has become active in bridging Hollywood to the Kazakhstan film industry.[^cite_note-339]

Kazakhstan journalist Artur Platonov won Best Script for his documentary "Sold Souls" about Kazakhstan's contribution to the struggle against terrorism at the 2013 Cannes Corporate Media and TV Awards.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341]

Serik Aprymov's *Little Brother* (*Bauyr*) won at the Central and Eastern Europe Film Festival [goEast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GoEast) from the German Federal Foreign Office.[^cite_note-EEC-342]

### Media

Main article: [Media of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Kazakhstan)



[Timur Bekmambetov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur_Bekmambetov), a popular Kazakh film director

Kazakhstan was ranked 161 out of 180 countries on the [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) [World Press Freedom Index](8264708.md) in 2014.[^cite_note-343] A mid-March 2002 [court order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_order), with the government as a [plaintiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaintiff), stated that *[Respublika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respublika_\(Kazakh_newspaper\))* were to stop printing for three months.[^cite_note-nytimes.com-344] The order was evaded by printing under other titles, such as *Not That Respublika*.[^cite_note-nytimes.com-344] In early 2014, a court also issued a cease publication order to the small-circulation *Assandi-Times* newspaper, saying it was a part of the Respublika group. Human Rights Watch said: "this absurd case displays the lengths to which Kazakh authorities are willing to go to bully critical media into silence."[^cite_note-345] [Mass media regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_regulation) and laws against [defamation](28661.md), [misinformation](2203174.md) and [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech) restrict the [freedom of speech](21401843.md).[^cite_note-a694-346]

With support from the US Department of State's [Bureau for Democracy, Human Rights and Labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Democracy,_Human_Rights,_and_Labor) (DRL), the American Bar Association Rule of Law Initiative opened a media support centre in Almaty to assist press outlets in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-aba-347]



[Mausoleum of Khoja Ahmed Yassaui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mausoleum_of_Khoja_Ahmed_Yasawi)

### Cuisine

Main article: [Kazakh cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_cuisine)

In the national cuisine, livestock meat, like [horse meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_meat)[^cite_note-348] and beef can be cooked in a variety of ways and is usually served with a wide assortment of traditional bread products. Refreshments include black tea, often served with milk and dried fruits (such as dried apricots) and nuts. In southern provinces, people often prefer [green tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_tea). Traditional milk-derived drinks such as [ayran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayran), [shubat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shubat) and [kymyz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kymyz). A traditional Kazakh dinner involves a variety of appetisers on the table, followed by a soup and one or two main courses such as [pilaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilaf) and [beshbarmak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beshbarmak). They also drink their national beverage, [kumys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumis), which consists of fermented mare's milk.[^cite_note-349]

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Kazakhstan](3959690.md)



[Astana Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana_Arena) opened in 2009.

Kazakhstan consistently performs in Olympic competitions. It is especially successful in [boxing](4243.md). This has brought some attention to the Central Asian nation and increased world awareness of its athletes. [Dmitry Karpov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Karpov) and [Olga Rypakova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olga_Rypakova) are among the most notable Kazakhstani athletes. [Dmitry Karpov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Karpov) is a distinguished decathlete, taking bronze in both the [2004 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics) and the [2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_World_Championships_in_Athletics) and [2007 World Athletics Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_World_Athletics_Championships). [Olga Rypakova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olga_Rypakova) is an athlete, specializing in [triple jump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_jump) (women's), taking silver in the [2011 World Championships in Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_World_Championships_in_Athletics) and Gold in the [2012 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics). [Mikhail Shaidorov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Shaidorov) won gold in the [2026 Winter Olympics](38416091.md) in Men's Figure Skating. Kazakhstan's city of [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) submitted bids twice for the [Winter Olympics](34069.md): in [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty_bid_for_the_2014_Winter_Olympics) and again for the [2022 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Winter_Olympics). [Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astana) and [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty) hosted the [2011 Asian Winter Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Asian_Winter_Games).[^cite_note-350]

Popular sports in Kazakhstan include football, basketball, ice hockey, bandy, and boxing. [Football](10568.md) is the most popular sport in Kazakhstan. The [Football Federation of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_Federation_of_Kazakhstan) is the sport's national governing body. The FFK organizes the [men's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_national_football_team), [women's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_women's_national_football_team), and [futsal](188197.md) national teams.



[Barys Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barys_Arena) in 2015

Kazakhstan's most famous basketball player was [Alzhan Zharmukhamedov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alzhan_Zharmukhamedov), who played for [CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBC_CSKA_Moscow) and the [Soviet Union's national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union's_national_basketball_team) in the 1960s and 1970s. [Kazakhstan's national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan's_national_basketball_team) was established in 1992, after the dissolution of the Soviet Union. Since its foundation, it has been competitive at the continental level. Its greatest accomplishment was at the [2002 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_at_the_2002_Asian_Games_%E2%80%93_Men), where it defeated the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_men's_national_basketball_team) in its last game to win the bronze medal. At the official [Asian Basketball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Basketball_Championship), now called FIBA Asia Cup, the Kazakhs' best finish was 4th place in 2007.

The [Kazakhstan national bandy team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_national_bandy_team) is among the best in the world, and has many times won the bronze medal at the [Bandy World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy_World_Championship), including the [2012 edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Bandy_World_Championship) when Kazakhstan hosted the tournament on home ice.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] The team won the first [bandy tournament at the Asian Winter Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy_at_the_2011_Asian_Winter_Games). During the Soviet time, Dynamo Alma-Ata won the [Soviet Union national championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_bandy_champions) in 1977 and 1990 and the [European Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Cup_\(bandy\)) in 1978. Bandy is developed in ten of the country's seventeen administrative divisions (eight of the fourteen regions and two of the three cities which are situated inside of but are not part of regions).[^cite_note-353]

The [Kazakh national ice hockey team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_men's_national_ice_hockey_team) competed at the [1998 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Winter_Olympics) and [2006 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Winter_Olympics), as well as the [2006 Men's World Ice Hockey Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Men's_World_Ice_Hockey_Championships). The [Kazakhstan Hockey Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_Hockey_Championship) has been held since 1992. [Barys Astana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barys_Astana) is the country's main professional ice hockey team and joined the [Kontinental Hockey League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kontinental_Hockey_League) in 2008–09. [Kazzinc-Torpedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazzinc-Torpedo) has played in the [Supreme Hockey League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Hockey_League) since 1996, while [Saryarka Karagandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saryarka_Karagandy) joined in 2012. Notable Kazakhstani players include [Nik Antropov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nik_Antropov), Ivan Kulshov, and [Evgeni Nabokov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evgeni_Nabokov). [Kazakh boxers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan_national_amateur_boxing_athletes) are well known internationally, having won more Olympic boxing medals than any country except [Cuba](5042481.md) and Russia across the last three Games. Three Kazakh boxers—[Vassiliy Jirov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassiliy_Jirov) (1996), [Bakhtiyar Artayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakhtiyar_Artayev) (2004), and [Serik Sapiyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serik_Sapiyev) (2012)—won the [Val Barker Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_Barker_Trophy) as the tournament's best boxer. At the [2000 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Summer_Olympics), [Bekzat Sattarkhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bekzat_Sattarkhanov) and [Yermakhan Ibraimov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yermakhan_Ibraimov) won gold medals, while [Bulat Zhumadilov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulat_Zhumadilov) and [Mukhtarkhan Dildabekov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhtarkhan_Dildabekov) won silver. Kazakh-born boxers have also achieved success internationally, including [Oleg Maskaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleg_Maskaev), who represented Russia and won the [WBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Boxing_Council) heavyweight title, and [Gennady Golovkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gennady_Golovkin), a multiple-time world middleweight champion. [Natascha Ragosina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natascha_Ragosina), born in [Qarağandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara%C4%9Fandy) and representing Russia, won multiple women's super middleweight and heavyweight titles and held records for the longest-reigning WBA and WBC female super middleweight champion.

### Public holidays

Main article: [Public holidays in Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Kazakhstan)

### UNESCO World Heritage sites

Kazakhstan has three cultural and two natural sites on the [UNESCO](21786641.md) World Heritage list. The cultural sites are:

- [Mausoleum of Khoja Ahmed Yassaui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mausoleum_of_Khoja_Ahmed_Yasawi), added in 2003

- Petroglyphs within the Archaeological Landscape of [Tamgaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamgaly), added in 2004

- [Silk Roads: the Routes Network of Chang'an-Tianshan Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Roads:_the_Routes_Network_of_Chang'an-Tianshan_Corridor), added in 2014

The natural sites are:

- [Saryarka - Steppe and Lakes of Northern Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saryarka_-_Steppe_and_Lakes_of_Northern_Kazakhstan), added in 2008

- Western [Tien Shan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tien_Shan), added in 2016

  [^cite_note-UNESCOkz-354]

[^cite_note-54]: &#x20;[Bregel, Yuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Bregel) (2003). [*An Historical Atlas of Central Asia*](https://archive.org/details/historicalatlasofcentralasiayuribregelbrill_642_T/page/60/mode/2up). Leiden; Boston: Brill. p. 76. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-90-04-12321-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-90-04-12321-2). The Qazaqs were, for the most part, not only the successors, but in many cases the direct descendants of the previous nomadic inhabitants of the Dasht-i Qïpchaq. Very often a direct continuity is seen between various Turkic and Mongol tribes that were found on this territory shortly before and immediately after the Mongol conquest, and various Qazaq tribes. Most of the conclusions about such continuity are based on the coincidence of some Qazaq and pre-Qazaq tribal names, especially when the tribes with these names were found on the same territory. Many Qazaq tribal names are found also among the Özbeks and the Qïrghïz, and these three peoples certainly have some common historical roots. The obvious historical predecessors of the Qazaqs on the territory of the Dasht-i Qïpchaq were the Qïpchaqs; the nomadic population of the Aq-Orda ("Golden Horde") was referred to as Qïpchaq, and the Qïpchaq tribe is among the tribes of the Qazaq Middle Horde (Orta Zhuz); but the names of the individual pre- Mongol Qïpchaq tribes seem to have no parallel among the tribes of the Qazaqs. On the other hand, the names of some important Qazaq tribes go back to the Mongol tribes that appeared in Central Asia with the Mongol conquest (and which were later Turkicized). This may be an indication that the tribes of Mongol origin were important components of the ethnic entity that was to be called Qazaq. Many Qazaq tribal names, mainly of the Middle and Senior Hordes, are the same as the names of Özbek tribes; this can be explained by their common origin in the Ulus of Orda

[^cite_note-55]: &#x20;Lee, Joo-Yup (2019). ["The Kazakh Khanate"](https://oxfordre.com/asianhistory/display/10.1093/acrefore/9780190277727.001.0001/acrefore-9780190277727-e-60). In Ludden, David (ed.). *The Oxford Research Encyclopedia of Asian History*. New York: [Oxford University Press](48518.md). [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/acrefore/9780190277727.013.60](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Facrefore%2F9780190277727.013.60). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-19-027772-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-19-027772-7). The Kazakh Khanate was a Chinggisid nomadic state that ruled the eastern Qipchaq Steppe (Dasht-i Qipchāq), a steppe zone that roughly corresponds to modern-day Kazakhstan, during the post-Mongol period as one of the most important successor states of the Mongol Empire and the last reigning dynasty of the Chinggisids. The Kazakh Khanate branched off from the Ulus of Jochi, whose people (ulus) were called Uzbeks in 15th-century Central Asia.

[^cite_note-56]: &#x20;Roman Pochekaev (2017). *Золотая орда. История в имперском контексте* (in Russian). Санкт-Петербург: «Наука». p. 206. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-5-02-039675-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-5-02-039675-3). Еще одно государство, явлвшееся наследником Золотой Орды, фактически просуществовало до середины XIX в. — это было Казахское ханство. Оно возникло в последней четверти XV в., довольно быстро распалось на ряд независимых владений, впоследствии трансформировавшихся в казахские жузы — Старший, Средний и Младший \[Another state that was a successor to the Golden Horde was the Kazakh Khanate, which in practice existed until the mid-19th century. It emerged in the last quarter of the 15th century and soon fragmented into a number of independent domains, which later developed into the Kazakh zhuzes — the Senior, Middle, and Junior zhuzes.]

Government of the Republic of Kazakhstan. Retrieved 6 August 2026.

[^cite_note-Sabitov-58]: &#x20;Sabitov, Zhaksylyk (2015). ["The Golden Horde — the "Stepdaughter" of Kazakh Historiography"](https://moluch.ru/archive/104/23260/). *Molodoy uchenyy* (in Russian). **104** (24): 842–851.

[^cite_note-59]: &#x20;Sultanov, Tursun; Kadyrbayev, Alexander (1997). "The Golden Horde". In Baipakov, Karl M.; Kozybayev, Manash K.; Kumekov, Bulat E.; Pishchulina, Klavdiya A. (eds.). *Istoriya Kazakhstana (s drevneyshikh vremen do nashikh dney) v pyati tomakh* \[*History of Kazakhstan (from Ancient Times to the Present Day) in Five Volumes*] (in Russian). Vol. 2. Alma-Ata: Atamura Publishing House. p. 624. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [5-7667-4695-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/5-7667-4695-4). Казахская государственность, несомненно, развилась на собственной основе и явилась плодом возрожденных традиций древнетюркской государственности. Золотая Орда — наглядное тому подтверждение. Можно говорить о неоднозначности воздействия Золотой Орды на ход истории, в первую очередь истории Казахстана и России, многообразии, противоречивости и протяженности во времени ордынских влияний, породившие, в свою очередь, споры в отечественной и мировой историографии о значении периода Золотой Орды для истории указанных стран \[Kazakh statehood undoubtedly developed on its own foundations and was a product of the revival of ancient Turkic traditions of statehood. The Golden Horde provides a clear example of this continuity. The impact of the Golden Horde on the course of history — particularly the histories of Kazakhstan and Russia — can be viewed as complex and multifaceted. The diversity, contradictions, and long-term nature of the Horde's influences have, in turn, generated debates in national and international historiography regarding the significance of the Golden Horde period for the history of these countries.]

[^cite_note-60]: &#x20;[Favereau, Marie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Favereau) (20 April 2021). [*The Horde: How the Mongols Changed the World*](https://books.google.com/books?id=jKMbEAAAQBAJ). Harvard University Press. p. 306. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-674-24421-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-674-24421-4). It is not by chance that the Jochid style of rule outlived the Horde in much of its former territory. The Manghit-Nogays, Tatars, Uzbeks, Qazaqs, and other heirs of the Horde kept nomadism alive, practicing its approaches to consensus, lineage, hierarchical sharing, and mobility not because these peoples were hide-bound traditionalists or ignorant of the ways of settled peoples but because these approaches were proven to work.

[^cite_note-61]: &#x20;Lee, Joo-Yup (2023). [*The Turkic Peoples in World History*](https://www.taylorfrancis.com/books/mono/10.4324/9781003256496/turkic-peoples-world-history-joo-yup-lee?refId=1f4be406-cec7-4025-87fd-1c867e1293fe\&context=ubx). New York: Routledge. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.4324/9781003256496](https://doi.org/10.4324%2F9781003256496). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-003-25649-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-003-25649-6). The Qazaqs are direct descendants of the Jochid ulus (people), who began to be called Uzbek after the reign of Uzbek Khan (r. 1313–1341), the Jochid ruler who made Islam the state religion of the Ulus of Jochi (the Golden Horde). The Jochid ulus, hence the Qazaqs, arose from the merging of the Mongols and various Inner Eurasian groups in the thirteenth century in the Qipchaq Steppe.

[^cite_note-62]: &#x20;Lee, Joo-Yup (April 2019). ["Turkic Identity in Mongol and post-Mongol Central Asia and the Qipchaq Steppe."](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/338291378). In Ludden, David (ed.). *The Oxford Research Encyclopedia of Asian History*. New York: Oxford University Press. p. 16. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/acrefore/9780190277727.013.443](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Facrefore%2F9780190277727.013.443). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-19-785173-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-19-785173-9). Among Western historians of Central Asia, Beatrice F. Manz, the leading specialist in Timurid history, addressed in detail the nature of the Turkic identity of the Mongol successors, including the Timurids. In her discussion of "the Turko-Mongolian world" in which Temür came to power, Manz convincingly demonstrates that the Turkic Muslim nomad elites of post-Mongol Central Asia and the Qipchaq Steppe exhibited a clear Mongol orientation.159 She thus defines Temür as "at once Muslim, Turk and Mongol"160 and views such new Turkic-speaking group identities of the post-Mongol era as the Chaghatays (Timurids), the Qazaqs, and the Uzbeks as divisions of Mongol identity.161 A similar view of these Turkic group identities has been taken by Bert G. Fragner, a leading Austrian Iranologist. In his article dealing with the Ilkhanid legacy in Iran, Fragner remarks that both the Islamized, Turkic-speaking Chaghatay ulus in the Timurid state and the Islamized, Turkic-speaking Jochid nomads of the Qipchaq Steppe possessed a strong Mongol self-identity

[^cite_note-63]: &#x20;[Favereau, Marie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Favereau); Pochekaev, Roman (2023). "The Golden Horde, c. 1260–1502". In Biran, Michal; Kim, Hodong (eds.). [*The Cambridge History of the Mongol Empire*](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/cambridge-history-of-the-mongol-empire/golden-horde-c-12601502/194D20494453E0AC8373BE9ADFB8B8D6). [Cambridge University Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_University_Press). pp. 243–318. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1017/9781316337424.005](https://doi.org/10.1017%2F9781316337424.005). Now many Muslim peoples living in the Russian Federation and Central Asia see the time of the Golden Horde as a foundation of their history. Most of the origin narratives of the Kazakhs, Turkmens, Bashkirs, Karakalpaks, Tatars, and other Muslim peoples are entangled in the conversion stories of the Jochid rulers and stem from the Golden Horde period ... In the sixteenth century, Jochi's offspring contributed to the creation of several khanates in Crimea and Central Asia, giving rise to smaller but resilient powers. In order to survive they adopted diverse strategies; either they sought the protection of larger neighbors such as the Ottoman Empire, Muscovy, and Poland–Lithuania, or they integrated a stronger nomadic formation such as the Noghai Horde, the Uzbeks, and the Kazakh khanate. These were expansionist powers led by both Chinggisid descendants and members of the local aristocracy, and their vitality was mainly due to the inﬂux of manpower from the lower Volga. Even if they stood outside the Jochid core territories, Uzbek and Qazaq carried high the Golden Horde legacy and used it as a springboard to attract warriors and conquer more peoples and cities. While in the west the Giray had very limited possibilities of expansion and were soon sedentarized, in Central Asia a new socioeco- nomic dynamic was keeping the nomadic society ﬂuid and in motion.

For its part, the Assembly might decide, already at this stage, to interpret Rule 59 of its Rules of procedure, concerning special guests, in such a way as to include the Eurasian States. Two Council of Europe member States, Turkey and Russia, belong geographically to both Europe and Asia and are therefore Eurasian. Strictly speaking, the three South Caucasus States, Armenia, Azerbaijan and Georgia are located in Asia, yet their membership of political Europe is no longer in doubt. \[...] This being the case, Kazakhstan, as a Eurasian State participating in the OSCE and a signatory to the International covenant on civil and political rights and the International covenant on economic, social and cultural rights, would meet the criteria laid down in Rule 59, making it eligible to apply for special guest status with the Assembly.
